Theorem 7.6 (Uniform joint asymptotic). Let C(n, d, k, δ) be the supremal unit-cube positive-
monomial ratio in dimension at most n, degree at most d, incidence treewidth at most k, and with
every mean at least δ. Assume integers n, d ≥ 2, k ≥ 1, and 0 < δ ≤ 1/2. Put q = min{n, d, 1/δ} ≥
ee , ϕ(q) = log q/ log log q, and h = min{k, ϕ(q)}. Along every joint parameter sequence with h → ∞,

                                               C(n, d, k, δ) ∼ h.

The same statement holds for the two-sided strip and with degeneracy or the orientation parameter
replacing treewidth.
Proof. The separate dimension, degree, marginal-floor, and incidence estimates give C ≤ min{(1 +
o(1))ϕ(q), k + o(k)}. Their relative errors vanish uniformly along the stated sequences, since both q
and k then diverge. For a simultaneous lower construction take

                             L = ⌊h⌋,           b = ⌊(q/2)1/L ⌋,           m = bL .

                                                          31
Then b ≥ (1 − o(1)) log q, L/b ≤ (1 + o(1))/ log log q → 0, and eventually b ≥ L ≥ 2. The radix
family has dimension m + L ≤ q/2 + ϕ(q) ≤ q ≤ n, degree m/b + 1 ≤ q ≤ d, and width L ≤ k. Its
smallest anchor mean and leaf failure mean are 1/m ≥ 2/q ≥ δ, and all other means also lie in the
two-sided strip. Thus its ratio L/[1 + (L − 1)/b] ∼ h. Its degeneracy and orientation parameter are
at most its treewidth, proving the alternatives. This is not an asymptotic assertion for a fixed small
value of k.


8     Feedback variables and frequency two
8.1    A universal repair law
The next argument works on the original factor scopes, including on boxes with positive lower
endpoints.
Lemma 8.1 (Feedback repair and gluing). Fix singleton Bernoulli means and a feedback variable
set F of size fF . For any nonnegative local functions ge and any prescribed-mean local laws Pe on
their scopes, there is one global prescribed-mean law P ′ satisfying

                                  EP ′ ge ≥ 2−fF EPe ge     for every e.

Proof. Extend each local law to F ∪ e by independently sampling any missing feedback coordinates.
Put C = 2fF . Construct a global reference law Q with a common uniform U : each feedback
coordinate independently chooses, fairly, its success interval [0, xj ] or [1 − xj , 1]; every outside
coordinate uses [0, xi ]. Write QF for its feedback marginal and Qi for its (F, i) marginal. For a
feedback state s and outside state b, every prescribed-mean local law satisfies

      P (s, b) ≤ m(s, b; i) := min {xj : sj = 1}, {1 − xj : sj = 0}, {xi if b = 1; 1 − xi if b = 0} .
                                                                                                      


The minimum includes all feedback coordinates and the outside coordinate. Choose the feedback
orientations that place every desired event at the left endpoint if b = 1, and at the right endpoint if
b = 0. This single orientation pattern has probability 1/C, and its intersection has length m(s, b; i).
Therefore Qi (s, b) ≥ P (s, b)/C. The same endpoint argument gives QF (s) ≥ PF (s)/C, also when
there are no outside variables. For F = ∅ its unique state has mass one.
    For a fixed extended factor law Pe , define the nonnegative residuals

            hs = QF (s) − (Pe )F (s)/C,      ri,s,b = Qi (s, b) − (Pe )F,i (s, b)/C   (i ∈ e \ F ).

For each i, b ri,s,b = hs . At state s with hs > 0, place residual mass hs and sample these outside
            P

coordinates independently with probabilities ri,s,b /hs . For hs = 0 all these residuals vanish. For
an empty outside scope place just the mass hs . The resulting measure Re has total mass 1 − 1/C,
and Pe′ = Pe /C + Re has feedback marginal QF and (F, i) marginal Qi , while dominating Pe /C
entrywise.
    Condition on any s with QF (s) > 0. The factor laws Pe′ (· | s) have consistent outside singleton
marginals Qi (s, b)/QF (s). Their incidence graph is a forest. Root each nontrivial component at a
factor, sample its law, then traverse the tree. Each newly reached factor shares exactly one already
sampled variable; sample its new variables using its local conditional law given that variable. Null
conditioning states are never encountered. Isolated variables use their stated conditional means;
factors with no outside variables need no sampling. This preserves every conditional factor law. Mix
over s with weights QF (s). The resulting global law has marginals Pe′ on F ∪ e, and nonnegativity
gives the claimed domination of expectations. There is one loss C, regardless of the lengths of the
forest paths.

                                                    32
Theorem 8.2 (Feedback-variable gap). For positive monomials on any finite nonnegative box,
deletion of fF variable vertices to an incidence forest implies T ≤ 2fF H.
Proof. Substitute fixed coordinates first. In normalized endpoint variables Y , an original physical
product is φe (Y ) = A⊆e cA i∈A Yi with cA ≥ 0. This expansion is used only inside the original
                     P       Q

scope. For each nonempty A choose an index kA of smallest normalized mean, and let

                                     ℓe (Y ) = c∅ +
                                                       X
                                                               cA YkA .
                                                      ∅̸=A⊆e

On binary vertices ℓe ≥ φe , while every prescribed-mean law has Eℓe = cav φe , by common upper
attainment. Thus ge = ℓe − φe is a nonnegative function on the original scope whose local maximum
expectation is the original factor’s exact gap. Apply Lemma 8.1 to maximizing local laws, then sum
with the positive coefficients. The full upper value is the sum of local upper values, proving the
result. Replacing the factors by expanded hyperedges would change the incidence graph and would
not justify this proof.

    For fF = 0 this recovers classical incidence-forest exactness; exactness of standard linearization
on Berge-acyclic hypergraphs is established in Del Pia and Khajavirad [15]. The general domination
lemma is sharp for arbitrary nonnegative local payoffs: on fF feedback bits and one outside bit,
all fair, use the 2fF +1 indicators of their states. Every local maximum is 1/2, while their sum is
identically one. Private variables fixed to one can make the scopes distinct. These indicators have
zero literals, so they do not prove sharpness for positive monomials when fF > 1.
Proposition 8.3 (One feedback variable is sharp). For n ≥ 2, let f (a, x) = a ni=1 xi + ni=1 xi
                                                                                     P           Q

and evaluate at a = 1/n, xi = 1 − 1/n on the unit cube. Then T = 2 − 1/n and H = 1. Its incidence
graph has treewidth two and becomes a tree after deleting a.
Proof. The bilinear terms contribute one in total and the large product contributes (n − 1)/n. If R
counts failed leaves and A is the binary anchor, the full deficiency is E[AR] + P(R ≥ 1) − 1/n, with
ER = 1 and EA = 1/n. Pointwise AR + 1{R ≥ 1} ≤ R + A, giving the upper bound one. Exactly
one uniformly chosen failed leaf and an independent anchor attain equality. Write e0 for the large
factor and ei for axi . Path bags {a, e0 , xi } with attached bags {a, xi , ei } give width two. A cycle
rules out width one, and deleting a leaves a tree.

8.2   Half-integral degree slabs
For frequency at most two, form a loopless dual multigraph with factor vertices. A shared variable
is an edge between its two factors; a private variable is an edge to its own new dummy vertex.
Parallel edges are allowed. Unused variables may be completed independently. Dummy factors have
zero weight or identically zero costs. Let g be its odd girth, with g = ∞ in the bipartite case.
Lemma 8.4 (Degree-slab vertices). For a loopless multigraph and p ∈ [0, 1]E , put sv (p) =
                                                                                             P
                                                                                               i∈δ(v) pi .
Every vertex of
                  S(p) = {z ∈ [0, 1]E : ⌊sv (p)⌋ ≤ sv (z) ≤ ⌈sv (p)⌉ (v ∈ V )}
has fractional edges forming vertex-disjoint odd cycles, all valued 1/2.
Proof. Let J be the fractional edges at a vertex and R the tight degree rows incident to them,
counting a repeated lower/upper equality once. The restricted rows must have full column rank on
J: otherwise a small perturbation in a null direction preserves every tight constraint and box bound,
contradicting extremality. Hence |J| ≤ |R|. An integer tight residual degree cannot contain exactly

                                                  33
one fractional edge, so every such row has at least two fractional incidences. Thus 2|R| ≤ 2|J|.
Equality forces each fractional edge to have both endpoints in R, and each such vertex to have
fractional degree two. These are cycles; an even cycle admits an alternating perturbation and is
excluded. On an odd cycle the two incident fractional values sum to the integer one, forcing all
values to 1/2. The argument excludes parallel fractional two-cycles and degree-one dummy vertices
automatically.

   This is the usual fractional matching rank argument, here stated for integer degree slabs; compare
the fixed-degree formulation in Barrus [6, Theorem 2.1].

Theorem 8.5 (Frequency two and odd girth). For positive monomials of frequency at most two on
a unit cube,
                            g
                      T ≤       H (g < ∞),        T = H (g = ∞).
                           g−1
Every finite odd-girth constant is sharp, and the unrestricted frequency-two supremum is 3/2.

Proof. Use failure probabilities pi = 1−xi , and define bv (p) = maxi∈δ(v) pi and cv (p) = min{1, sv (p)},
with empty values zero. For a failure edge set J, write Iv (J) for its coverage indicator. Exact
single-product envelopes give

                     T =       av (cv − bv ),   H=      max        av (EIv (J) − bv ).              (8.1)
                           X                                  X

                           v                         P(i∈J)=pi v


In particular an approximation of unshifted coverage alone is insufficient. Decompose p = EZ
into vertices of S(p). On each allowed slab cv is affine (linear below one, constant above), so
Ecv (Z) = cv (p); convexity gives Ebv (Z) ≥ bv (p).
    Given Z = z, keep integral edges. On each fractional odd cycle of length l, choose one of
its l maximum matchings uniformly, then fairly choose that matching or its complement in the
cycle. Each edge has marginal 1/2. A matching leaves its uniformly selected vertex uncovered; its
complement covers every vertex. Thus coverage probability is 1 − 1/(2l) at each cycle vertex. A
vertex with another selected integral edge is covered surely. Otherwise its cv (z) = 1 and bv (z) = 1/2.
All vertices off these cycles have equal integral bv (z) = cv (z). With α = 1 − 1/g, this proves

                                 E[Iv (J) | Z] ≥ αcv (Z) + (1 − α)bv (Z).

Averaging retains the baseline: EIv (J) − bv (p) ≥ α[cv (p) − bv (p)]. Sum this in (8.1). For a bipartite
graph there are no fractional cycles, so the slab decomposition itself attains cv (p) simultaneously,
proving exactness.
    For sharpness take a unit-weight odd cycle of length l with all edge means 1/2. Its termwise gap
and coverage baseline both equal l/2. For every selected edge set J, v Iv (J) ≤ |J| + (l − 1)/2: if
                                                                          P

|J| ≤ (l − 1)/2 use 2|J|, otherwise use l. Expected coverage is therefore at most l − 1/2, attained by
the matching/complement law. The hull gap is (l − 1)/2.

    Bipartite equality is a statement about the scalar envelopes. It does not assert compatibility of
all separately feasible product-value coordinates in the full lifted multilinear polytope. Zero-lower
boxes transfer by scaling; unequal positive aspect ratios need a separate analysis.




                                                   34
8.3    Convex cardinality factors
A discrete sequence φ(0), . . . , φ(d) is convex if φ(k + 2) − 2φ(k + 1) + φ(k) ≥ 0. Its cardinality factor
is the unique multiaffine interpolant of the values φ( i Xi ) on binary vertices. It is generally not the
                                                         P

literal continuous composition φ( i xi ). Sequences may be negative, decreasing, or nonmonotone;
                                      P

only their discrete convexity is needed.

Lemma 8.6 (Cardinality envelopes). Let fe be such a factor and φbe its piecewise-affine interpolation
between integers. Then                              X 
                                   vex fe (p) = φbe     pi .
                                                             i∈e

A prescribed-mean law supported on the two adjacent cardinalities attains this minimum. One
common-threshold law maximizes every such factor and their sum. If p(1) ≥ · · · ≥ p(d) are the local
means, its upper value is
                                             d
                                  φe (0) +       [φe (j) − φe (j − 1)]p(j) .
                                             X

                                             j=1

Proof. Jensen’s inequality gives the lower bound for the convex function φbe . The cube slab between
the two adjacent integer sums is integral: two fractional coordinates admit an opposite perturbation;
one fractional coordinate cannot make an integer sum bound tight and admits a small perturbation.
Decompose p into its binary vertices to attain equality. Discrete convexity implies supermodularity
of the cardinality set function. Among laws maximizing a factor, choose one maximizing also
E|X|2 . If two incomparable support sets have positive mass, replace equal mass on them by their
intersection and union. Singleton means are unchanged; supermodularity cannot decrease the
primary value, while the secondary value strictly increases by twice the product of the two nonempty
set differences’ sizes. This is impossible. The maximizer is chain-supported, and the chain law
with prescribed means is precisely the common-threshold law. The same law therefore maximizes
each factor individually, and summing establishes simultaneous upper attainment. Integrating its
cardinality gives the displayed formula.

   The single-factor principles are classical; symmetric envelope formulas appear in Sherali [37],
and the common-aspect product formula in Adams et al. [1, Proposition 4.1].

Theorem 8.7 (Cardinality odd-girth bound). For a sum of convex cardinality factors of frequency
at most two, with each entire factor kept in the relaxation, the sharp constants of Theorem 8.5 hold
unchanged.

Proof. Use success probabilities in the degree slab, and decompose p = EZ as before. Each local
lower envelope is affine on its allowed slab, so

                             E vex fv (Z) = vex fv (p),         ET (Z) ≤ T (p),

where the second assertion also uses concavity of each upper envelope. At a fractional-cycle vertex
v, let m count its incident integral ones. Its local lower value is φv (m + 1), and its upper value
is [φv (m) + φv (m + 2)]/2, since precisely two coordinates are half-valued. Matching/complement
rounding on a length-l cycle selects zero or two of them with probability 1/(2l) each, and one
otherwise. Therefore the expected local cost is exactly vex fv (Z) + Tv (Z)/l. Off the cycles the local
gap is zero. Averaging gives

                             vex f (p) ≤ Ef (X) ≤           vex fv (p) + T (p)/g.
                                                      X

                                                        v


                                                      35
Subtract from the common upper value. Bipartite slabs are integral and need no rounding. Positive
bilinear odd cycles prove sharpness.

Corollary 8.8 (Common-aspect physical products). For original positive products on boxes [ℓi , ρe ℓi ],
ℓi > 0, assume each scope e has a common coordinate aspect ratio ρe > 1. Then the frequency-two
odd-girth theorem applies to the original factors. Its constant is sharp for every fixed common ratio.
Proof. A normalized binary vertex gives the table ae ( i∈e ℓi )ρK e , which is discrete convex. Shared
                                                                 Q

variables enforce ratio compatibility, so different connected components may have different ratios.
No new factors are introduced. For a bilinear odd cycle on [1, ρ]n , expansion gives affine terms plus
(ρ − 1)2 times the unit-cube bilinear polynomial. Both gaps scale by the same positive number.
Fixed coordinates are substituted first, and ratio one is trivial.

8.4   Rational scalar-envelope algorithms
Proposition 8.9 (Frequency-two binary oracle). For a rational positive polynomial of frequency at
most two on a unit cube and arbitrary rational λ, one can minimize f (X) − λT X over binary X in
polynomial bit time.
Proof. With Z = 1 − X, the objective is

                                  λi +       λ i Zi +        av 1{v uncovered by Z}.
                              X          X              X
                          −
                              i          i              v

Dummy vertices have zero penalties. Include every negative-cost edge: adding it strictly lowers
cost and cannot increase an uncovered penalty. Add its cost to the constant, remove it from the
remaining choices, and set its endpoints’ penalties to zero, retaining those vertices. Remaining
costs and penalties are nonnegative. Add a hub h, its mate k, their zero-cost edge hk, and an
edge vh of cost equal to each vertex v’s penalty. Any selected original edge set extends to an edge
cover by paying exactly its uncovered penalties and adding hk. Conversely, retaining original edges
of an augmented cover leaves uncovered only vertices whose penalty edges were paid. These two
comparisons prove value equality with minimum-weight edge cover. For completeness this reduces
to matching: for a nonnegative-cost graph without isolated vertices let µ(v) be its cheapest incident
cost. From a matching M , add a cheapest edge at each unmatched vertex. Its cover cost is at most
  v µ(v) −   uv∈M (µ(u) + µ(v) − cuv ). Conversely an inclusion-minimal cover is a union of stars (a
P          P

path of three edges or a cycle would have a removable edge). Choose one edge from each star as
a matching. Every other edge can be charged to its unmatched leaf, with µ(v) no larger than its
cost. This shows the minimum of the displayed expression is no larger than the minimum cover
cost. Hence equality holds and maximum-weight matching supplies an optimal cover. Parallel edges
can keep their cheapest representative. Weighted matching is polynomial-time [17].

Proposition 8.10 (Convex-degree matching oracle). For explicitly listed rational discrete-convex
tables and arbitrary rational edge costs ci , the binary problem

                                         min       φv (degJ (v)) +
                                               X                     X
                                                                           ci
                                         J⊆E
                                               v                     i∈J

is solvable in polynomial bit time.
Proof. At vertex v create dv slots with nondecreasing charges ∆v (j) = φv (j) − φv (j − 1). For each
edge i = uv, create mandatory vertices iu , iv , joined by an inactive zero-cost edge. Connect iu to
all slots of u with cost ∆u (j) + ci , and iv to all slots of v with cost ∆v (j). A matching covering

                                                            36
all mandatory vertices either uses the inactive edge or uses two slots; mixed activation cannot
cover both endpoints. Thus it represents one binary choice per original edge. Every chosen set is
representable, and the occupied slots at a vertex can be reassigned to its cheapest k slots because
their available gadget neighbors are identical. Their charges telescope to φv (k) − φv (0).
    To enforce mandatory coverage by ordinary unconstrained matching, let W be the sum of
absolute original gadget edge costs and subtract M = 2W + 1 per mandatory endpoint covered by
each edge. The all-inactive matching covers every mandatory vertex. Missing even one such vertex
loses bonus M , exceeding any possible original cost difference. Thus an optimum covers them all,
also with negative increments or negative ci . Adding v φv (0) and undoing the fixed bonus recovers
                                                     P

the desired value. The gadget has O(|E|) vertices and O( v d2v ) edges with polynomial rational
                                                            P

encoding length. This is the incremental-slot matching mechanism of convex degree optimization;
compare Deza and Onn [16, Theorem 1.2 and Section 3].

Corollary 8.11 (Exact scalar envelopes and supporting minorants). Both preceding factor classes
admit exact rational convex-envelope evaluation, a supporting affine minorant, and scalar graph-
hull separation in polynomial bit time. This includes rational common-aspect physical products.
No polynomial-size explicit description of the full polytope carrying all individual factor values is
asserted.

Proof. The finite vertex-law LP has dual

                           max{η + λT p : η + λT z ≤ f (z) (z ∈ {0, 1}n )}.
                            η,λ


Its separation oracle is precisely binary minimization of f (z) − λT z, with arbitrary signed linear
costs. Affine summands are absorbed into these costs and restored in the value; unused coordinates
optimize independently. Let A = v maxj |φv (j)|, or A = v av for positive monomials, adding the
                                  P                         P

absolute coefficient sum of separate affine terms if present. Then |f (z)| ≤ A. The dual polyhedron
contains no line, since the rows (1, z) span Rn+1 , and its attained optimal face contains a vertex.
Such a vertex solves n + 1 independent tight equations with a zero-one matrix. Cramer’s rule
bounds each coordinate by (n + 1)!A; its nonzero integer denominator determinant has magnitude
at least one. Clearing input denominators also gives polynomial bit length for each coordinate.
Thus the box of radius max{1, (n + 1)!A} retains an optimum and has polynomial encoding size.
Rational separation-to-optimization [23, Theorem 6.4.9] gives the exact optimum and minorant, also
at boundary means. Every vertex inequality extends to the whole cube by multiaffine interpolation.
Sorting gives the common-threshold upper value and a supporting upper affine function; together
with cube bounds these separate the scalar graph hull. Rational common-ratio products generate
their convex tables by polynomially many rational multiplications.


9    The exact incidence-treewidth-two constant
Theorem 9.1 (One-sided cycle coloring). In a finite simple bipartite graph G = (V, F ; I) of treewidth
at most two, one can color the factor side F with two colors so that every cycle whose factor vertices
all have one color contains an even number of factor vertices. Consequently every zero-one matrix
with this bipartite support has a partition of its rows into two totally unimodular matrices.

    The all-cycle assertion is stronger than excluding induced odd-factor cycles (balancedness). The
stronger statement will supply total unimodularity for arbitrary integer cardinality slabs.



                                                 37
Proof. We prove a two-terminal series-parallel invariant. A network starts with an edge; series com-
position identifies one terminal of each of two otherwise disjoint networks, and parallel composition
identifies both terminal pairs. Intermediate networks are simple and bipartite subgraphs of the
eventual graph. A coloring is good if its monochromatic-factor cycles have even factor count. A
terminal path is c-monochromatic when all its factor vertices, including factor terminals, have color
c. Its parity counts those factor vertices modulo two.
    For each network there is a parity ϵ ∈ {0, 1} with all the following witnesses available. They are
possibly different colorings. For V, V terminals and either color c, an active good coloring has at least
one c-monochromatic terminal path, all such paths have parity ϵ, and no path is monochromatic in
the other color. If ϵ = 0, a blocking good coloring with neither type of monochromatic terminal
path is also available. For mixed terminals, with either prescribed color c of the factor terminal, an
active good coloring has paths of color c, all of parity ϵ; a blocking witness with that prescribed
terminal color is available whenever there is no direct terminal edge. For F, F terminals, with either
prescribed common color c, the same active witness exists; if ϵ = 1 a blocking witness with those
colors also exists. In addition, for either assignment of distinct terminal colors, a good coloring
exists. Such colors automatically block monochromatic terminal paths. Finally a network with a
direct terminal edge is mixed and uses ϵ = 1.
    The base edge satisfies the mixed invariant with parity one. For a series composition let δ be
one if the shared vertex is a factor, and zero otherwise. The output parity is ϵ = ϵ1 ⊕ ϵ2 ⊕ δ. Every
path concatenates component terminal paths; subtracting the shared factor once accounts for δ.
Every cycle stays in a component. Active witnesses concatenate by using the same desired color
and matching the shared factor’s color when present. The required additional witnesses have the
following complete case analysis.

  1. For outer types V, V and a shared variable, use opposite active colors in the two V, V
     components. No monochromatic path can cross both.

  2. For outer types V, V and a shared factor, blocking is required only if the component parities
     differ. The even mixed component has no direct edge and can block with the prescribed
     shared-factor color; use an active witness of that color in the other component.

  3. For mixed outer types and a shared variable, use the active color opposite to the outer factor’s
     color in the V, V component and use that prescribed factor color in the mixed component.
     This blocks.

  4. For mixed outer types and a shared factor, give the shared factor the color opposite to the
     outer factor. The F, F component uses its distinct-terminal witness, and the mixed component
     uses a compatible active witness. This again blocks.

  5. For outer types F, F of common color and a shared variable, blocking is required only for
     output parity one, so the two mixed component parities differ. Block the even component,
     which has no direct edge, and activate the other, respecting both outer colors.

  6. For outer types F, F of common color and a shared factor, color the shared factor oppositely.
     Both F, F components have their required distinct-terminal good witnesses and block.

  7. For outer F, F terminals of distinct colors and a shared variable, activate each mixed component
     in its outer terminal’s color. If instead the shared vertex is a factor, give it either color and
     use in each component the equal-color active or distinct-color witness as appropriate.



                                                   38
These cases include reversed mixed types and exhaust all triples of terminal types. A nontrivial
series composition has no direct outer edge, so its mixed blocking requirement has also been checked.
     In a parallel composition every new simple cycle consists of one terminal path from each
component. If the paths are monochromatic in one color, its factor parity is their parity XOR plus
τ , the number of factor terminals modulo two. Thus τ = 0 for equal terminal types and τ = 1 for
mixed types. We check the invariant as follows. For V, V terminals with equal component parities,
activate both in the same color. If both are even, both may also block. For different parities,
activate the odd component and block the even component. Output parity is ϵ1 ∨ ϵ2 ; whenever
both components contribute paths their equal parities make new cycles even. For F, F terminals of
common prescribed color, activate both when parities agree; if both are odd, both may also block.
If parities differ, activate the even component and block the odd one. Output parity is ϵ1 ∧ ϵ2 .
With distinct prescribed terminal colors, use their good witnesses in both components; a crossing
monochromatic cycle would contain both differently colored terminals and is impossible. For mixed
terminals with no direct edge, both components can block in the prescribed factor color. Activate
either one and block the other for an active witness, choosing its parity as output, or block both
for the blocking witness. For mixed terminals with a direct edge, simplicity implies exactly one
component contains it. Activate its parity-one witness and block the edge-free component. No
blocking output is required. These mixed choices create no crossing monochromatic cycle. The
induction is complete.
     A treewidth-two graph has no K4 minor, hence no subdivision of K4 . The classical block
characterization supplies a two-terminal series-parallel representation for each nontrivial block [24,
Theorem 3.1]. Apply the invariant to each block. Root the block-cut forest. If a child block attaches
at a factor, interchange its two colors if needed to match the articulation color; if it attaches at a
variable no matching is needed. Bridges use the base edge and isolated factors receive arbitrary
colors. Every cycle lies in one block, proving the graph assertion.
     For the matrix assertion use Camion’s criterion: a 0, ±1 matrix is totally unimodular exactly
when every Eulerian submatrix has entry sum divisible by four [13, Theorem 6.5]. In either color
class an Eulerian submatrix has even row and column degrees. Its bipartite support decomposes
into edge-disjoint simple cycles. Each has an even number of factor vertices, hence length divisible
by four. Its total number of ones is consequently divisible by four, as required. The argument covers
rectangular Eulerian submatrices too.

Theorem 9.2 (Sharp width-two gap). A sum of discrete-convex cardinality factors with incidence
treewidth at most two satisfies T ≤ 2H. This includes positive monomials on unit cubes and
zero-lower boxes, and original positive products with a common aspect ratio within each scope. The
sharp supremum is two, including for every fixed common positive aspect ratio ρ > 1.

Proof. Partition factor rows into two TU classes by Theorem 9.1. For a class C and prescribed
means p, the polytope
                                        jX      k                  lX         m
                        0 ≤ z ≤ 1,                                                (e ∈ C)
                                                      X
                                               pi ≤         zi ≤         pi
                                         i∈e          i∈e          i∈e

is integral and contains p. Indeed row sign changes, duplicate rows, and unit rows preserve total
unimodularity; each basic solution with integer right-hand side is integral by its determinant ±1.
A binary-vertex decomposition of p attains all class lower envelopes by Lemma 8.6. Under that
law every other factor has nonnegative expected deficiency from its own upper value. The equal
mixture of the two class laws therefore attains at least half the sum of local gaps. Their common
upper extremizer makes this a lower bound for half the full termwise gap in the scalar hull gap.


                                                    39
    For positive unit-cube monomials one can equivalently use failure rows i∈e zi ≤ 1 when their
                                                                                               P

mean sum is at most one and i∈e zi ≥ 1 otherwise. A classwise integral decomposition attains
                                 P

the failure union bound in every row. The mixed unit-right-hand-side integrality theorem for
balanced matrices suffices for this particular argument [13, Theorem 6.13]; it would not justify
arbitrary integer cardinality slabs without the stronger TU conclusion. Common-aspect products
are cardinality factors by Corollary 8.8. Proposition 8.3 proves the unit-cube lower supremum two.
    For sharpness at a fixed positive ratio, put ϵ = 1/ρ and α = 1 − ϵ. On [ϵ, 1]n+1 use
                                          n            n

                 fn (a, x) = α                  xi +                     EA = 1/n,    EXi = 1 − 1/n,

where a = ϵ + αA and xi = ϵ + αXi are endpoint variables. The normalized nonlinear bilinear terms

                   Tn = 2α −            ,              Hn = max E[αAR + 1 − ϵR ] −                 .

For any M > 0, 1 − ϵr ≤ αr on integer r ≥ 0 gives

          E[1 − ϵR ] ≤ α(1 − E[R; R > M ]) + 1/M,                        αE[AR] ≤ αM/n + αE[R; R > M ].

Markov’s inequality was used in the first bound. Thus Hn ≤ α + αM/n + 1/M . Exactly one

uniformly failed leaf and an independent anchor give Hn ≥ α + α/n − (1 − ϵn )/n. Taking M = n
yields Hn → α and Tn /Hn → 2. The incidence graph is the same width-two graph as before; all
means are interior. Scaling physical coordinates by ρ maps the box to [1, ρ]n+1 and preserves the
gaps of the correspondingly rescaled positive polynomial.

9.1   What fails for general nonnegative factors
Example 9.3 (An exact width ratio of three). Use six fair variables in pairs AB, BC, CA, and put

                      E(u, v) = 1 − u − v + 2uv,                         N (u, v) = u + v − 2uv.

These are the multiaffine equality and inequality indicators, both in [0, 1] on the square. Define

             gA = E(AB)E(CA),                    gB = N (AB)E(BC),                   gC = N (BC)N (CA).

Each local upper envelope is one at the center: mix a satisfying assignment and its complement.
Each local lower envelope is zero, by the analogous mixture of a falsifying assignment. No two
factors can equal one on a binary assignment, because adjacent factors demand opposite parities on
their common pair. Thus the sum’s upper envelope is at most one, attained by the all-zero/all-one
mixture, which satisfies gA . The pair assignment AB = 01, BC = 01, CA = 00 and its complement
make all factors zero, proving the sum’s lower envelope zero. Therefore T = 3 and H = 1 for these
three entire factors.
    A central bag {A, B, C} and six attached bags {A, B, XAB,j }, {B, C, XBC,j }, {C, A, XCA,j },
j = 1, 2, form a width-two decomposition. A four-cycle proves the width is exactly two. Every
variable has frequency two. The factors are nonnegative on the full cube, but their monomial
coefficients have mixed signs, so neither the positive-monomial nor the convex-cardinality factor
theorem applies.


                                                                    40
Proposition 9.4 (Independent-set payoff family). For a simple graph G with nonnegative vertex
weights wv , place two fair bits on each edge and assign its endpoints opposite demanded parities.
Give vertex v payoff wv if all its incident demanded parities hold, and zero otherwise. The sum of
separate maximum expected payoffs is v wv , while the global maximum is αw (G), the maximum
                                         P

independent-set weight. With all constructed factor scopes retained, including zero-weight factors, a
graph with an edge has incidence treewidth max{2, tw(G)}.

Proof. Each local satisfaction pattern and its complement attain wv with fair means. Satisfied
vertices form an independent set, since the two endpoints of an edge demand opposite parities.
Conversely fix a maximum independent set, give each incident edge its selected endpoint’s demand,
and choose remaining parities arbitrarily. Complement-pair mixing satisfies all selected factors with
fair means, proving the maximum αw (G); any extra satisfied factors still form an independent set
and cannot improve that maximum. To a decomposition of G attach two size-three bags {u, v, Xuv,j }
for every edge at a bag containing u, v. This proves the treewidth upper bound. Contracting one
length-two path per edge and deleting the other gives G as a minor; each edge also creates a
four-cycle, proving both lower bounds.

   Thus Kk+1 with unit vertex weights gives maximum-payoff ratio k + 1 at incidence width k for
k ≥ 2. A general payoff maximum ratio is not an envelope-width ratio: the minima must also be
computed. Example 9.3 does compute them and gives the claimed exact width obstruction.

9.2   Limits of the coloring method
A distinct open question at incidence treewidth two asks whether the factors admit two classes
whose incidence subgraphs are each chordal bipartite, meaning they contain no induced cycle of
length at least six. The proved all-cycle parity property permits induced eight-cycles and does
not establish this target. The recorded experiment found such a partition for all 5,000 generated
supports; this is finite evidence only.
    The all-cycle coloring invariant cannot extend with a bounded number of colors to width three.
In K3,m , m ≥ 3, with its three-vertex side interpreted as variables, every three factors lie on a
six-cycle. Each permitted color class therefore contains at most two factors, requiring ⌈m/2⌉ colors,
although the graph has treewidth three. Its all-ones matrix is already TU: determinants of order at
least two vanish. The six-cycles have chords and do not obstruct balancedness. This separates the
stronger coloring mechanism from a TU-row-partition target.
    Finite searches found three balanced classes in 3,000 generated width-at-most-three supports
(212 required more than two), and three TU classes in 300 further supports. Their accepted widths
were certified by particular elimination orders; rejected greedy orders did not certify larger width.
The TU tests enumerated column signings by the Ghouila-Houri criterion and used exact coloring
backtracking. These searches are finite evidence, not proofs of universal partitions. The exact Wk
for k ≥ 3, k-class balanced or TU partitions, and sharp planar constants remain open here. A
planar bipartite incidence graph does admit an orientation of maximum outdegree at most two:
Hall’s theorem assigns every edge to one of two slots at an endpoint, since every edge set uses
at most twice as many edges as incident vertices. Orient from the assigned endpoint. Thus the
general orientation theorem gives a finite planar bound, without settling its sharp value. Auxiliary
reductions and the simpler forest-coloring obstruction are recorded in Appendix D.




                                                 41
10     Positive boxes and coefficient regularity
Let Cbox (ρB ) be the supremal gap ratio for positive monomial factorizations on strictly positive
boxes with every coordinate aspect ratio ui /ℓi ≤ ρB , over arbitrary dimensions and evaluation
points with positive hull gap. Unlike Cδ , this restricts the box, not the normalized evaluation point.
Fixed coordinates are removed.

10.1    A coefficient inequality with one ambient orientation law
Fix an ambient dimension N ≥ 2. Choose a uniform subset J ⊆ [N ] of size ⌊N/2⌋ and an independent

Call this balanced orientation law O. It has all prescribed means, though for odd N its individual
orientation choices are not fair. The probability that a fixed pair has opposite orientations is

Lemma 10.1 (Finite spreading inequality). For every support S ⊆ [N ], every mean vector, and
every integer j ≥ 0,
                        Pj − Vj ≤ βN (Cj − Oj ) + (Cj−1 − Pj−1 ).                      (10.1)
The same statement holds with βN replaced by 2 and independently fair endpoint orientations in
every coordinate.

Proof. Write β for the indicated constant and set FS,j = βCj + Vj − Pj − βOj + Cj−1 − Pj−1 .
Orders zero and one vanish; order d + 1 is Cd − Pd ≥ 0, and higher orders vanish. For order two
let L2 = a<b max(0, pa + pb − 1). Equal orientations give a pair’s upper Fréchet value; opposite
          P

orientations give its lower value. Their opposite probability is 1/β, including under restriction. Thus

                                 FS,2 = (C2 − P2 ) + (V2 − L2 ) ≥ 0.

The first inequality follows from upper optimality, the second because every law, including an
adjacent-cardinality law, respects every pair lower bound.
    We induct on support size. A mean-zero coordinate is deterministic and its deletion gives
FS,j = FS\{i},j . A mean-one coordinate and Pascal’s identity give FS,j = FS\{i},j + FS\{i},j−1 .
The adjacent-cardinality values obey the same identity after shifting the sum by one. These are
restrictions of the ambient law, not newly sampled balanced laws, so its correlated coins cause no
exception. All boundary points follow by induction; dimension one is immediate.

Theorem 10.2 (Positive-box upper bounds). For N ≥ 2 nonfixed coordinates and ρB > 1, every
positive multilinear polynomial on a strictly positive box of aspect ratios at most ρB satisfies

                                   T ≤ (ρB + βN )H ≤ (ρB + 2)H.

The common mixture of independence and balanced orientation with weights ρB /(ρB + βN ) and
βN /(ρB + βN ) captures this fraction of each original monomial’s exact gap simultaneously. For zero
or one nonfixed coordinate all gaps vanish.
Proof. First consider a support on [1, ρB ]N and put t = ρB − 1. Its binary physical value is
(1 + t)K , so its upper, independent, orientation, and lower values are C(t) = j Cj tj , P (t), O(t),
                                                                                  P

and V (t) = j Vj tj . The last identity follows from a single adjacent-cardinality law attaining every
             P

order together. The coefficient of tj in (βN + t)C + V − (1 + t)P − βN O is FS,j ≥ 0. Consequently
the true local gap satisfies
                                 C − V ≤ ρB (C − P ) + βN (C − O).                              (10.3)
The same mixture works on all supports in the ambient N coordinates. Summing with nonnegative
coefficients and using common upper attainment proves the common-aspect conclusion. Fair
independent orientation coins and β = 2 prove ρB + 2 directly in every dimension.

                                                     43
   For unequal endpoints 0 < ℓi < ui put

                        ui /ℓi − 1
                 si =              ∈ (0, 1],         zi = ℓi [(1 − si ) + si yi ],   yi ∈ [1, ρB ].
                         ρB − 1
This is an affine bijection onto the original box. Each original monomial becomes a positive sum
of physical y-monomials. Its true factor gap is at most the sum of their gaps, by (2.3), while the
full hull gap is affine invariant. Apply the common-aspect bound to the expansion, always using
restrictions of the same ambient orientation law. Moreover the original factor’s upper value is the
sum of the expanded upper values by common-threshold attainment. Its deficiency under either
law is thus the sum of expanded deficiencies, proving the claimed per-original-factor guarantee.
The certificate can be sampled without forming the expansion: the endpoint laws pull back to the
original endpoints. Box inclusion alone would not prove any of these comparisons.

Theorem 10.3 (Coefficient-regularity cardinality bound). In ambient dimension N ≥ 2, suppose
each entire local factor is the multiaffine interpolant of
                             de

also handles L = 0, deterministic means, and affine factors. With at most one nonfixed coordinate
the interpolants are affine and all gaps vanish. The product sequence ρkB has aj = (ρB − 1)j , so
L = ρB − 1 recovers the common-aspect product inequality.

    This refinement is a direct consequence of the coefficient mechanism; no optimality or independent
priority is asserted. Distinct earlier low/high and asymmetric proofs, and the precise limitation of
fixed independence/orientation mixtures, appear in Appendix E.

10.3    Unequal aspect ratios at frequency two
Example 10.5 (A rational bipartite ratio 7/6). For f (x, y, z) = xy + xyz on [1, 2] × [1, 2] × [1, 3],
evaluate at (5/4, 5/4, 5/2). Put x = 1 + a, y = 1 + b, z = 1 + 2c, so the normalized mean is
(1/4, 1/4, 3/4). The following valid affine bounds give exact values:

         xy ≥ 1 + a + b,    xyz ≥ 2a + 2b + 3c,          f ≥ 4a + 4b + 4c,            f ≤ 2 + 8a + 4b + 2c.

                                                         45
Validity follows by substitution at all eight binary vertices and multiaffine interpolation. The
respective attaining distributions are
                  xy lower     000, 001, 011, 101 each of mass 1/4
                  xyz lower    001 of mass 3/4; 110 of mass 1/4
                   f lower     001 of mass 1/2; 011, 100 each of mass 1/4
                   f upper     000 of mass 1/4; 001 of mass 1/2; 111 of mass 1/4.
Each row has the required means. The separate upper bounds xy ≤ 1+2a+b and xyz ≤ 1+6a+3b+2c
are attained by the last law. Thus T = 13/2 − 19/4 = 7/4, while H = 13/2 − 5 = 3/2. The dual
multigraph is bipartite: its two factor vertices share two parallel edges, with a dummy leaf for z.
This disproves unequal-box bipartite scalar exactness, while leaving the common-aspect theorem
intact.
Proposition 10.6 (Bipartite positive-box boundary). For arbitrary nonnegative boxes with a
bipartite dual graph, positive monomial gaps satisfy T ≤ 2H. Their worst ratio over strictly positive
boxes is at least 3/2, already with two factors and three variables. Its exact value between 3/2 and 2
remains open here, as does a universal 3/2 bound for unequal-box frequency-two instances.
Proof. More generally a proper coloring of the term conflict graph into q classes gives T ≤ qH:
terms in one class have disjoint scopes, so join their minimizing laws independently and complete
unused means. They attain their entire class gap, and other factors have nonnegative expected
deficiency from their upper envelopes. Since those upper values are commonly attained, H is at least
each class’s total gap. Sum these inequalities. This also proves a bound two for any two positive
terms regardless of their boxes or frequency.
    For the lower family let 0 < ϵ ≤ 2 and use fϵ = (2/ϵ)xy + xyz, with x, y ∈ [1, 1 + ϵ], z ∈ [1, 3], at
normalized means (a, b, c) = (1/4, 1/4, 3/4). After removing affine parts the two factors are
                  A = 2ϵab,      B = ϵ2 ab + 2ϵac + 2ϵbc + 2ϵ2 abc,               C = A + B.
Their lower bounds at the point are attained at the first three laws of Example 10.5, respectively.
The affine certificates are A ≥ 0, B ≥ ϵ2 (a + b + c − 1), and C ≥ 2ϵ(a + b + c − 1). In binary order
000, 001, 010, 011, 100, 101, 110, 111, the last two residual lists are
                               (ϵ2 , 0, 0, ϵ(2 − ϵ), 0, ϵ(2 − ϵ), 0, ϵ(ϵ + 4)),
                               (2ϵ, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0, ϵ2 , ϵ(3ϵ + 2)),

which are nonnegative throughout the stated range. The local lower values sum to ϵ2 /4 and the full
lower value is ϵ/2. The same common upper law as before gives 3ϵ/2 + 3ϵ2 /4. Hence
                                                                  T   2(ϵ + 3)
                  T = ϵ(3/2 + ϵ/2),      H = ϵ(1 + 3ϵ/4),           =          −→ 3/2.
                                                                  H    3ϵ + 4
To obtain unit coefficients divide the polynomial by 2/ϵ and put z ′ = (ϵ/2)z. It becomes xy + xyz ′
on [1, 1 + ϵ]2 × [ϵ/2, 3ϵ/2], with the same ratio.

   For frequency-two products on unequal positive boxes, normalizing upper endpoints to one gives
a binary failure cost i∈J∩e αi = exp(− i∈J∩e wi ), where αi = ℓi /ui and wi = − log αi . Each
                       Q                     P

edge has the same weight wi at both incident factors. This is a weighted convex degree potential,
whereas the matching theorem above uses unweighted cardinalities. Finite floating-point searches
approaching 3/2 from below do not supply an upper bound. If the dual multigraph is a forest,
conditional gluing actually preserves all local minima and maxima even for signed multiaffine factors
on arbitrary boxes; Appendix D gives the elementary argument.

                                                      46
11     Exact complexity of a single physical product
A ratio bound can be small even when evaluating an individual factor’s exact envelope is difficult.
For one factor the ratio is identically one wherever its gap is positive. The next result concerns
computing that factor’s envelope, not loss caused by separating interacting terms. All complexity
statements use rational binary encodings and bit time.

Theorem 11.1 (Midpoint decision on narrow unequal boxes). Given rational ui > 1 and q, deciding

A partition state and its complement, with equal probabilities, have all means 1/2 and S = A/2

encoding length, since log A is polynomial in the original input length. For a fixed η > 0, choose
fixed K with 1/(16K) ≤ η to enforce the narrower interval.
     For NP membership, the vertex-law LP has equality rows 1, z1 , . . . , zn and right-hand side
(1, 1/2, . . . , 1/2). An optimal basic solution has at most n + 1 positive probabilities. It provides
that many binary states and rational weights of polynomial bit length: a nonsingular subsystem
has zero-one entries and half-integer right-hand side, so determinant bounds apply. Each vertex
product has polynomial bit length as a product of at most n input rationals. Exact arithmetic
verifies nonnegative weights, total mass, all means, and expected product at most q in polynomial
time.

common box. The separating accuracy can be exponentially small in input length.
Corollary 11.2 (Graph-hull membership). Deciding whether (x̄, q) belongs to the scalar graph hull
of the same single monomial is NP-complete under the same narrow-box restrictions.

proves membership.

Proposition 11.3 (A fixed number of ratio types). For one product on a rational strictly positive
box having at most k distinct coordinate aspect ratios, its exact scalar envelope and a supporting
affine minorant can be computed in nO(k) times a polynomial in the input bit length. In particular
fixed k is tractable.
Proof. After substituting fixed coordinates and normalizing endpoints, a binary product with count
                                             h
hj in ratio group j has value ( i ℓi ) kj=1 rj j . To minimize this value minus i πi zi for arbitrary
                                Q      Q                                         P

dual coefficients, sort the coefficients within each group. For fixed counts choose the hj largest
coefficients, and use prefix sums. Enumerate all j (nj + 1) ≤ (n + 1)k count tuples. Rational
                                                      Q

multiplication and sorting have polynomial bit cost; this is an exact vertex oracle. The determinant-
bounded dual argument of Corollary 8.11 then computes the lower envelope and its minorant. The
common-threshold upper envelope is explicit. Thus mere closeness of the ratios does not substitute
for a bound on their number of distinct values.

11.1    Rank-one transport and logarithmic accuracy dependence
Multimarginal optimal transport with specified finite marginals minimizes expected cost over their
joint laws. With n fair binary marginals and factored cost tensor
                                 n

                                 O                                         Y

                                 i=1                                        i

it is exactly the midpoint envelope above. This tensor is strictly positive and rank one, with no
sparse correction, and
                            1 ≤ Cz ≤ (1 + ϵai ) ≤     (ϵA)j ≤ 16/15.
                                     Y             X

                                            i                       j≥0


                                                           48
Corollary 11.4 (Rank-one value and weighted dual accuracy). Exact threshold evaluation for
these rank-one MOT instances is NP-complete. Unless P = N P , neither their values nor their
weighted vertex minima admit a deterministic additive-δ approximation algorithm polynomial in
rational input length and log(Cmax /δ). For randomized bounded-error algorithms the corresponding
consequence requires N P ̸⊆ BP P .

Proof. Take δ = ϵ2 /32 in the YES and NO intervals of Theorem 11.1. Their approximation intervals
remain disjoint and log(1/δ) is polynomial in the PARTITION input length. Such an algorithm
would decide PARTITION. The weighted vertex oracle is hard directly: set

                                         ϵ2
                            πi = ϵai +      (Aai − a2i ),     d0 = 1 − ϵ2 A2 /8.
                                         2
Equation (11.1) gives
                                               ϵ2
                              Cz − π T z = d0 +   (S(z) − A/2)2 + R(z).
                                               2
Its minimum is at most d0 + ϵ2 /8 in a YES instance and at least d0 + ϵ2 /2 in a NO instance. The
same approximation margin and polynomial encoding bounds apply. Exact MOT decision has the
NP certificates already given. If an input convention requires bit lengths polynomial in the number
of marginals, append a number of dummy binary factors (1, 1) linear in the original input bit length.
Give them fair marginals and zero dual potentials. They change neither rank, cost range, value, nor
oracle minimum, and meet that convention.

    The published structured-MOT paper of Altschuler and Boix-Adserà [3, Section 7.2, after
Theorem 7.4] asks whether constant-rank approximate dual minimization can have logarithmic
inverse-accuracy dependence, leading to exact algorithms. The preceding reduction rules out that
dependence in the rational bit model under the stated complexity assumption, already at rank one.
Their Corollary 7.5 has polynomial dependence on inverse accuracy and is consistent with it. This
comparison identifies the historical question; it is not a claim that no subsequent work addressed it.
Nor is it a lower bound in an unrestricted unit-cost arithmetic model. The unweighted smallest
entry of this tensor is simply one; arbitrary dual weights are essential. None of these arguments
proves hardness at fixed additive error.

11.2    A boundary of multilinearity
Example 11.5 (Two positive rational-power terms). On the fixed interval [1, 3], let fϵ (x) =
x1−ϵ + x1+ϵ , 0 < ϵ < 1. The first term is concave and the second convex, while

                               fϵ′′ (x) = ϵx−1−ϵ [(1 + ϵ)x2ϵ − (1 − ϵ)] > 0.

At x = 2 their separate widths and full width are consequently

         Tϵ = 3 sinh(ϵ log 3) − 4 sinh(ϵ log 2),       Hϵ = 1 + 3 cosh(ϵ log 3) − 4 cosh(ϵ log 2).

These follow by subtracting each function from its endpoint secant, with the opposite subtraction
for the concave term. Taylor expansion gives

            Tϵ = (3 log 3 − 4 log 2)ϵ + O(ϵ3 ),       Hϵ = 12 [3(log 3)2 − 4(log 2)2 ]ϵ2 + O(ϵ4 ).

The first leading coefficient is log(27/16) > 0; the second is positive by strict convexity of x(log x)2
on [1, 3]. Thus their ratio diverges like a positive constant times 1/ϵ. Taking ϵ = 1/k, k ≥ 2, keeps

                                                      49
rational exponents in [1/2, 3/2] with only two positive unit coefficients and one variable. Under
x = ey , however, both terms become convex exponentials on [0, log 3]. Their lower envelopes are
exact and endpoint secants add, so the termwise and full widths coincide in that representation. The
example concerns the original coordinate and does not preclude useful logarithmic reformulation.


12     Fractional cardinality and spatial certificates
The following optimization problems have explicit solutions. Their role is to distinguish the size of
certificates produced by specified spatial oracles from the difficulty of finding an optimizer. The
obstruction uses a concave quadratic penalty and an affine balance; it is a separate construction
from the positive multilinear gap examples above.
    Fix integers n ≥ 2 and 0 ≤ k ≤ n − 1, and write

        K = k + 21 ,       Fn,k = {x ∈ [0, 1]n :         i xi = K},            F (x) =    i=1 xi (1 − xi ).   (12.1)
                                                     P                                   Pn


The dimension parameter n counts original coordinates. A vertex of Fn,k has exactly k coordinates
equal to one, one equal to 1/2, and the others zero: two strictly fractional coordinates would admit
a small opposite perturbation, whereas a point with at most one fractional coordinate is determined
by its active bounds and the balance. Concavity and compactness therefore give

                                                     min F = 14 .                                             (12.2)
                                                     Fn,k

For each oracle below, a certified region is either infeasible or has oracle bound at least T∗ . A
certificate is a finite cover of Fn,k by such regions, as in Section 2.3. For absolute tolerance ϵ ≥ 0
we grant the best incumbent and set T∗ = 1/4 − ϵ. Pruning at U − ϵ, for any feasible incumbent
U ≥ 1/4, implies this condition. Infeasible regions contain no witness and do not help cover the
feasible set. All lower bounds also hold for a weaker valid oracle: certifying a region with that oracle
certifies it with the stronger oracle used in the proof.
    There are close precedents in other certificate models. Jarre’s binary knapsack construction
retains an exponential branch-and-bound obstruction with a max-cut semidefinite bound [26, Sections
2–3]. Coniglio’s hyperplane-clustering analysis gives a related spatial obstruction under midpoint
branching on symmetric coordinate domains [12, Assumption 1, Propositions 2–4, Appendix C in
the review version]. Our arguments concern arbitrary coordinate restrictions and a fixed objective
gap under the explicitly defined node oracles. We make no priority claim for exponential spatial
lower bounds in general. The fractional-cardinality moments used later are classical; the additional
argument places them in spatial regions and counts how many witnesses a certified region can
contain.

12.1    Chords and endpoint avoidance
For a box B =     i [ai , bi ] ⊆ [0, 1] , including degenerate intervals, define

with +∞ for an empty feasible intersection. If gi is any convex underestimator of xi (1 − xi ) on

                                                            50
the infimum for any separable convex underestimator i gi is at most LBch (B); an infimum is used
                                                              P

because arbitrary convex underestimators need not attain their infima on a closed interval without
lower semicontinuity at its endpoints.
     For clarity, two common formulations yield exactly these chords. For yi = 1 − xi ∈ [1 − bi , 1 − ai ],
both McCormick lower planes for wi = xi yi become wi ≥ ℓi (xi ). The upper planes remain compatible:
the exact product lies above the chord and below each upper plane. The separable αBB expression
xi (1 − xi ) − α(xi − ai )(bi − xi ) has second derivative 2(α − 1), so the smallest convexifying value α = 1
gives the chord. Larger valid values give weaker underestimators. These statements concern the
specified separable formulations, without additional coupled cuts or nonlinear subproblem solution
rules.
     We isolate the counting argument so that later oracle changes do not obscure it. Let n = k+m+z,

12.2    Upper certificates, tightening, and tolerances
Proposition 12.3 (Explicit upper certificates). For 0 < ϵ < 1/4, a chord certificate for (12.1)    has
at most 2n+2 − 3 nodes and 2n+1 − 1 leaves. For ϵ = 0, a midpoint certificate has exactly n+1
                                                                                              
                                                                                          k+1   leaves
if only the stopping rules in the proof are used. In particular its node count is O(nmin{k+1,n−k} )
when that exponent is fixed.

Proof. Choose 0 < α < 1/2 with α(1 − α) = 1/4 − ϵ. Process coordinates in order. At each surviving
node split the next coordinate at α, then split its upper child at 1 − α. The middle interval has
constant chord α(1 − α), and all other chords are nonnegative, so the middle child is certified.
Continue on the low and high intervals. At a final corner box the chord sum is (1 − α)∥x − v∥1 ,
where v ∈ {0, 1}n is its corner. On the demand slice, ∥x − v∥1 ≥ |K − i vi | ≥ 1/2. This gives
                                                                             P

a bound (1 − α)/2 > 1/4 > T∗ . Processing coordinate i adds four nodes per surviving prefix, of
which there are 2i−1 . The stated counts follow. For a strict pruning rule choose a slightly larger
middle-chord target still below 1/4.
    For exact certification split at 1/2 instead. If a prefix has c high intervals and d low intervals,
its chord bound is at least each of
                                      c−K           K − (n − d)
                                          ,                     .
                                       2                 2
Indeed, the sum on high coordinates is at most K, whereas the sum on low coordinates is at least
K − (n − d); all other chord contributions are nonnegative. Stop at c = k + 1 or d = n − k. No
path survives depth n. With remaining high and low stopping counts a, b, the number of leaves
satisfies L(a, b) = L(a − 1, b) + L(a, b − 1) and L(0, b) = L(a, 0) = 1, hence L(a, b) = a+b
                                                                                          a . Start at
(a, b) = (k + 1, n − k). A full binary tree has twice as many leaves minus one nodes. This proves the
exact count and polynomial assertion.

    The balanced fixed-tolerance family therefore has certificate size 2Θ(n) under the chord model.
If both k and n − k are proportional to n, choose m, z proportional to n − k to obtain the same
exponential order. Fixed demand level, or fixed complementary demand level, admits the polynomial

                                                    52
certificate just proved. At ϵ ≥ 1/4, the root chord bound zero already meets the target; no positive
exponent is asserted there.
    The chord error is genuinely quadratic in interval width:
                                                      1X               n
                     0 ≤ F (x) −         ℓi (xi ) ≤       (bi − ai )2 ≤ max(bi − ai )2 .                 (12.8)

In particular, the difference between the exact node optimum and the chord bound is at most this
quantity, by evaluating F at a chord minimizer. Thus the fixed-tolerance lower bound persists
despite this second-order error estimate; it does not require a first-order local approximation error.

Proposition 12.4 (Charged reductions and equality tolerance). Suppose a run keeps feasible points
covered by its final boxes together with discarded boxes certified by the same oracle at T∗ . If it
processes N nodes and discards at most a such boxes per node, a cover lower bound L implies
N ≥ L/(a + 1). Pure feasibility reductions need not be charged when they discard no feasible point.
    For a demand feasibility tolerance 0 ≤ δ < 1/2, replace Fn,k by Fn,k
                                                                      δ = {x ∈ [0, 1]n : | P x −K| ≤
                                                                                            i i
δ}. Its exact optimum is 1/4 − δ 2 . If ϵ + δ 2 < 1/4, a chord certificate at absolute tolerance ϵ for
this enlarged set satisfies Theorem 12.2 with ϵ replaced by ϵ + δ 2 .

Proof. The augmented cover has at most (a + 1)N members. A discarded open coordinate slab may
be charged by its closed box only when that box is certified; this condition is part of the assertion,
not an unproved closure assumption. Subsequent product-domain results can charge the removed
coordinate set itself.
    For a fixed sum s ∈ [k + 1/2 − δ, k + 1/2 + δ] ⊂ (k, k + 1), the same vertex argument as above
gives minimum penalty (s − k)(1 − s + k). Its minimum on this interval is 1/4 − δ 2 , attained by k
ones and a remaining coordinate 1/2 − δ or 1/2 + δ. A tolerance-feasible incumbent is therefore at
least this value. The exact-balance witnesses remain feasible for the enlarged problem, and the chord
bound over the enlarged feasible set is at most its value at each witness. The proof of Theorem 12.2
applies at the positive target 1/4 − (ϵ + δ 2 ).

   The restriction δ < 1/2 is material: once the tolerance interval contains an integer sum, the
optimum is zero. An unrestricted formula 1/4 − δ 2 would then be false. Relative pruning in the
convention LB ≥ (1 − θ)U , 0 ≤ θ < 1, on the original problem implies the absolute target with
ϵ = θ/4.

Theorem 12.6 (SDP–RLT cover bound). Let n = k + m + z, where k, m, z ≥ 1 are integers, and
let 0 ≤ ϵ < 1/4. Every cover for (12.1) certified by (12.9)–(12.10) at T∗ = 1/4 − ϵ has at least N (q)
members, where q = min{k, z, m(1/2 − 2ϵ)}. In the balanced case k = m = z = t, this is the chord
lower bound in Theorem 12.2.
Proof. If a containing box has |R| < q, Lemma 12.5 gives a feasible objective at most |R|p(1 − p) <
q/(2m) ≤ T∗ , where the strict comparison holds also when R = ∅ because q > 0. The box cannot
be certified. Therefore |R| ≥ q on every certified box containing a witness, and Lemma 12.1 applies.
The diagonal mixed inequality gives Xii ≤ (ai + bi )µi − ai bi , so the SDP bound dominates the chord
bound. The upper certificates of Proposition 12.3 remain valid.
    This resolves the behavior under spatial branching for this SDP–RLT model; it is not merely a
calculation of its root value.
    One can also grant all products of two affine inequalities valid on B ∩ Fn,k without strengthening
this oracle. Indeed, linear-programming duality represents any such nonnegative affine function, on
a nonempty polytope, as a nonnegative constant plus a nonnegative combination of box slacks plus
a multiple of e(x) = i xi − K. The representation is an identity of affine polynomials, including for
                      P

degenerate boxes. Expanding the product of two representations leaves only existing nonnegative
slack moments and terms L[ev] = 0 with v affine. This assertion concerns affine inequalities in the
original x coordinates. It does not grant all valid affine cuts in the lifted (µ, X) coordinates.

    At r = 1 this model is exactly (12.9)–(12.10): positivity on affine squares is augmented PSD,
products of at most two slacks give box RLT, single slacks give first-moment bounds, and the
balance times affine polynomials gives its first and second moment equations. At every order r ≥ 1
the model dominates that degree-two model and hence the chord oracle. Certification again means
an infeasible region or LBr (B) ≥ T∗ = 1/4 − ϵ for problem (12.1). Original dimension remains n.

13.1    A self-contained positivity proof
The moments below are the classical fractional-cardinality moments associated with Grigoriev’s
knapsack obstruction [22]. Potechin gives the explicit construction and its classical provenance [35,
Theorem 1, Example 18, Theorem 44]. We prove the precise sufficient range required for the present
full preordering; no external positivity theorem is needed.
    For real v and integer a ≥ 0, write (v)a = v(v − 1) · · · (v − a + 1), with (v)0 = 1. In s formal
Boolean variables u1 , . . . , us , let red replace every positive power of ui by ui . This is the canonical
multilinear representative modulo u2i − ui ; it respects sums and products modulo these identities
and cannot increase degree. For uS = i∈S ui define
                                              Q

All subset sizes are at most s, so denominators are positive. The functional is defined on the
Boolean quotient and is used only through the degree stated in each assertion; no positivity on all
polynomials is assumed. In particular Es,t [1] = 1. For s = 0, deterministic evaluation of constants
will be used instead of a fractional-cardinality functional.

           v + 2d ≤ 2r,    s − v ≥ 2d,       t − a ≥ 2r − 1 − a ≥ 2d − 1,         s − t − b ≥ 2d − 1.

Lemma 13.1 applies to Es−v,t−a . This proves positivity, including for arbitrary squares that couple
all unassigned variables and for repeated original slacks.

    The sufficient degree range in Lemma 13.1 is not the sharp classical range for the moment
matrix alone. For the full preordering and this particular functional, the slack products explain the
two-sided restriction: if 0 < t < s is noninteger, s ≥ 2r, and t < 2r − 1, a product of ⌊t⌋ + 2 ≤ 2r
distinct lower slacks has a negative falling-factorial moment. Applying the same argument to upper
slacks treats s − t < 2r − 1. Integer t is different: (13.4) is then expectation under the uniform
distribution of t-subsets. Sharper moment-matrix positivity alone therefore does not extend this
full-preordering construction past the noninteger slack-product obstruction.

13.2    The order versus region tradeoff
Theorem 13.3 (Full-preordering spatial cover bound). For problem (12.1), let n = k + m + z with
positive integers k, m, z, let r ≥ 1 be an integer, and let 0 ≤ ϵ < 1/4. Put

                           qr = min{k − 2r + 2, z − 2r + 2, m(1/2 − 2ϵ)}.                               (13.10)

If qr > 0, every box cover certified by (13.1)–(13.3) at T∗ = 1/4 − ϵ has at least N (qr ) members. If
qr ≤ 0, only the trivial bound of one region is asserted.



                                                           57
Proof. Take a box containing a witness (12.4) and suppose |R| < qr . Since |R| and k − 2r + 2 are
integers, |R| < k − 2r + 2 implies |H ∩ U | ≥ k − |R| ≥ 2r − 1; likewise |Z ∩ U | ≥ 2r − 1. Set
                               s = |U |,      t = |H ∩ U | + p|M ∩ U |.
Then t, s − t ≥ 2r − 1. Also s ≥ 2r: for r = 1 a unit and a zero coordinate remain, and for
r ≥ 2 we have s ≥ 4r − 2 ≥ 2r. Define L by fixing xR = wR and applying Es,t to xU = u. This
degree-preserving substitution is normalized. It changes e into i∈U ui − t, whose products with
                                                                P

every substituted multiplier of degree at most 2r − 1 vanish by Lemma 13.1. Bound slacks on R
are nonnegative constants, and those on U are Boolean slacks. Removing constant factors cannot
increase degrees, so Lemma 13.2 proves every required positivity constraint.
    The Boolean penalty on U vanishes, leaving
                                                                qr
                       L[F ] = |M ∩ R|p(1 − p) ≤ |R|p(1 − p) <     ≤ 14 − ϵ.            (13.11)
                                                               2m
The strict inequality follows from |R| < qr and qr > 0, including R = ∅. This feasible functional
precludes certification. Thus every certified box containing a witness has |R| ≥ qr , and Lemma 12.1
proves the bound. In particular non-strict pruning at the target is covered: the constructed value is
strictly below it. No integer rounding of qr /2 is necessary.

    For balanced parts k = m = z = t, positivity of qr is precisely r < (t + 2)/2 together with
ϵ < 1/4. A uniform exponential bound as n = 3t → ∞ follows whenever ϵ < 1/4 is fixed and r ≤ ct
for a fixed c < 1/2. At ϵ = 1/8, the exponent n/24 survives whenever
                        t − 2r + 2 ≥ t/4,      equivalently      r ≤ 3t/8 + 1.
An order approaching the positivity boundary can instead give only a constant qr ; positivity alone
is not an exponential-size assertion. When ϵ = 1/4, the root has an available certificate because
the mixed slacks give L[F ] ≥ 0. We do not divide by T∗ or by a vanishing qr in any of these cases.
Increasing r may itself make the oracle costly; the theorem grants that oracle and counts regions
only.
    The affine-inequality closure following Theorem 12.6 extends to this degree. Expand a product
of valid affine inequalities in their nonnegative box-slack and balance representations and multiply
by an allowed square. Terms with a balance factor vanish within degree 2r, and all remaining terms
are existing preordering inequalities. This still grants no arbitrary coupled nonlinear valid cut, no
localizer of an objective cutoff, and no analytic solution of the coupled problem. The charged-cover
convention of Proposition 12.4 applies to objective-based spatial reductions.

13.3    Unique minimizers and increasing concave costs
Corollary 13.4 (Distinct perturbations). Let 0 < d1 < · · · < dn and         i di ≤ η, where η > 0. On
                                                                           P

Fn,k replace F by Fd = F + i di xi . Its unique optimizer has
                          P

                      x∗i = 1 (i ≤ k),      x∗k+1 = 1/2,      x∗i = 0 (i ≥ k + 2),             (13.12)
with value Fd∗ = 1/4 + i≤k di + dk+1 /2. For the cover assertion take n = k + m + z with positive
                       P

integers k, m, z and integer r ≥ 1, as in Theorem 13.3. Under the order-r box oracle, a cover at
T∗ = Fd∗ − ϵ has at least N (qr,η ) members provided 0 ≤ ϵ, ϵ + η < 1/4, and
                    qr,η = min{k − 2r + 2, z − 2r + 2, m(1/2 − 2ϵ − 2η)} > 0.                  (13.13)
There is no nonidentity coordinate permutation preserving both feasible set and objective, even when
objective equality is required only on the demand slice.

                                                  58
Proof. The Hessian of Fd is −2I, so a nonvertex, lying strictly between two distinct feasible points,
cannot minimize this strictly concave function. All vertices have base value 1/4. Among their linear
costs, an exchange puts the largest coordinate value at the smallest coefficient: if i < j and xi < xj ,
exchanging them reduces cost by (dj − di )(xj − xi ) > 0. Hence the unique minimizing vertex is
(13.12). It also uniquely minimizes the linear cost over the whole slice: if i < j, xi < 1, and xj > 0,
transferring min{1 − xi , xj } > 0 from coordinate j to coordinate i strictly decreases that cost. At a
linear-cost minimizer no such pair remains, so the balance forces exactly (13.12).
    The node constraints themselves impose 0 ≤ L[xi ] ≤ 1, since xi and 1 − xi are nonnegative
combinations of node slacks and constants. The constructed functional therefore gives linear cost at
most η. If |R| < qr,η , its total value is strictly below

                              m(1/2 − 2ϵ − 2η)
                                               + η = 1/4 − ϵ ≤ Fd∗ − ϵ,
                                    2m
so the previous count applies.
    The slice has an interior point relative to its balance hyperplane, namely xi = K/n ∈ (0, 1). A
linear form constant on this slice has coefficient vector proportional to the all-ones vector. If a
coordinate permutation preserves the objective there, the permuted coefficient vector differs from d
by a constant vector. Comparing sums forces this constant to be zero, and distinctness forces the
identity permutation.

    An explicit choice is di = 2ηi/[n(n + 1)], whose sum is η. For n = 3t, ϵ = 1/8, and η = 1/32,
the lower bound is (3/2)n/32 whenever r ≤ 13t/32 + 1. If η < ϵ, the chord certificate at tolerance
ϵ − η has base bound at least 1/4 + η − ϵ ≥ Fd∗ − ϵ; the nonnegative linear term preserves it. Thus
this explicit perturbed family again has 2Θ(n) certificate size under the specified oracles.
    Adding the conserved flow gives increasing strictly concave coordinate costs:

                      Cd (x) =        [xi (2 − xi ) + di xi ] = Fd (x) + K   (x ∈ Fn,k ).       (13.14)
                                 X

                                 i

Each derivative is 2−2xi +di > 0 on [0, 1] and each second derivative is −2. Every equality-preserving
functional adds exactly K, so the optimizer and all absolute-gap conclusions are unchanged. When
k is proportional to n, this raises the optimum to order n, while the unshifted obstruction has
constant size. A fixed relative tolerance cannot be inferred from that absolute bound. Section 15
uses fixed-dimensional blocks to address precisely this distinction.


14     Arbitrary coordinate domains and local graph lifts
The endpoint argument uses membership, not interval geometry. We first strengthen the information
available about each original coordinate, then show that adding arbitrarily many auxiliaries depending
on that coordinate does not cost degree in this particular moment construction.

14.1    All valid local polynomial information
Let S = i Si , where each Si is an arbitrary nonempty subset of [0, 1]. Closedness, connectedness,
         Q

semialgebraicity, and a finite description are not required. Define the order-r product-domain oracle
by normalized functionals on R[x]≤2r , all balance products (13.2), and all inequalities
                                         
                               a
                                     gj  P 2  ≥ 0,            deg gj + 2 deg P ≤ 2r,           (14.1)

                              j=1                           j


                                                       59
where each gj is any univariate polynomial nonnegative on its coordinate set Si . The empty product

                                  L[hv] = 0                (deg h + deg v ≤ 2r)                   (14.2)

for every univariate polynomial h vanishing on its Si and every polynomial multiplier v in the

The node bound is the infimum of L[F ] or L[Fd ], as appropriate. A certified cover has original
feasible set Fn,k , original dimension n, and target 1/4 − ϵ or Fd∗ − ϵ respectively.

Theorem 14.1 (Product-domain certificates). Under the respective parameter assumptions of
