E.3    Optimality within a fixed two-law mixture
Proposition E.3 (A limitation of fixed independent/fair-orientation mixtures). Fix ρ > 1 and an
orientation weight w ∈ [0, 1]. For mixtures of I and independently fair O, the best uniform per-
physical-monomial captured fraction over all dimensions is at most min{w/2, (1 − w)/ρ}. Optimizing
the weight gives exactly 1/(ρ + 2), attained at w = 2/(ρ + 2).

Proof. For a bilinear product at normalized means (ϵ, 1 − ϵ), 0 < ϵ < 1/2, deleting affine parts
gives DI /T = ϵ and DO /T = 1/2. Letting ϵ ↓ 0 bounds the mixture fraction by w/2. For the
other obstruction use means ϵ and k copies of 1 − ϵ/k, where k ≥ 1. Divide physical values by
ρk+1 , and write α = 1/ρ, η = 1 − α, γ = (1 + α)/2. The sum of success means is k, giving V = α.
Common-threshold and fair-orientation integration, split at ϵ/k and ϵ, and direct independent
multiplication give

                                      C = α + ϵ[η − α(1 − αk )/k],
                                      P = α[1 + (ρ − 1)ϵ][1 − ηϵ/k]k ,
                                      O = α + ϵ[η − 2γ(1 − γ k )/k].

For clarity, in the orientation law the two end intervals of length ϵ/k have normalized expected
product γ k+1 , the two next intervals of total length 2(ϵ − ϵ/k) have value γ, and the middle interval
of length 1 − 2ϵ has value α; these yield the stated O. For each fixed k, divide deficiencies by ϵ and
let ϵ ↓ 0. Then

                                     T /ϵ = η − α(1 − αk )/k,
                                    DI /ϵ −→ αη − α(1 − αk )/k,
                                   DO /ϵ = [2γ(1 − γ k ) − α(1 − αk )]/k.

The denominator coefficient is at least η 2 > 0 because 1 − αk ≤ kη. Now let k → ∞ to obtain
DI /T → 1/ρ and DO /T → 0, bounding the mixture by (1 − w)/ρ. The maximum of the minimum
of these two bounds is 1/(ρ + 2) at their intersection. Lemma 10.1 with fair orientations supplies
the matching guarantee.


                                                      92
   This is only an optimality statement for a fixed two-law mixture’s per-monomial guarantee over
unrestricted dimensions. Other laws, coefficient-dependent mixtures, and the true optimum joint
law for a sum of monomials are outside it. In particular it does not prove Cbox (ρ) = ρ + 2, nor
conflict with the finite-ambient balanced-orientation refinement.


F     A classical point-packing benchmark
The following four values were conjectured by Anstreicher [4, Section 4] and proved by Khajavirad
[28, Propositions 1(i–ii) and 3]. We include a variance proof and explicit feasible covariances to
illustrate how an exact relaxation value differs from a spatial certificate bound. For n ≥ 2 points
(xi , yi ) ∈ [0, 1]2 , maximize γ subject to (xi − xj )2 + (yi − yj )2 ≥ γ for i < j. Replace the two sets of
products by symmetric matrices X, Y and write

                            Dij = Xii − 2Xij + Xjj + Yii − 2Yij + Yjj ≥ γ.

RLT imposes all four products of coordinate-bound slacks, including repeated indices, as in (12.10).
SDP instead imposes X ⪰ xxT , Y ⪰ yy T and the diagonal secants Xii ≤ (ℓi + ui )xi − ℓi ui , and
likewise for Y . The symmetry bounds, denoted SYM, restrict xi ∈ [1/2, 1] for i ≤ nx = ⌈n/2⌉ and
yi ∈ [1/2, 1] for i ≤ k = ⌈n/4⌉. Reflection gives the lower-half convention in Khajavirad’s paper.
Both RLT and SDP secants are recomputed on these intervals; adding only first-moment bounds
would define different models.

Proposition F.1 (Four exact values). The optimal values are

                                   model                 value     range
                                   RLT                     2       n≥2
                         SDP (with or without RLT)     n/(n − 1)   n≥2
                               RLT + SYM                  1/2      n≥5
                      SDP + SYM (with or without RLT) k/[4(k − 1)] n ≥ 5.

Here k − 1 = ⌊(n − 1)/4⌋.

Proof. For two coordinates in a common interval [ℓ, ℓ + L], the RLT maximum of Xii − 2Xij + Xjj
at fixed means is
                          L min{xi + xj − 2ℓ, 2ℓ + 2L − xi − xj } ≤ L2 .
Take both diagonals at their secants and the off-diagonal at the larger of its two lower McCormick
planes. This is feasible for all pairs at once: the diagonal secant dominates both diagonal lower
planes, and each pair’s lower planes lie below its upper planes. Thus the bound two holds without
symmetry and is attained at x = y = 1/2, with diagonal 1/2 and off-diagonal zero in each
matrix. With symmetry, two of the first k ≥ 2 points give the bound 1/2. Put the three groups
Q = [k], H = [nx ] \ Q, and F = [n] \ [nx ] at (3/4, 3/4), (3/4, 1/2), and (1/2, 1/2). Set every
diagonal to its interval secant and each off-diagonal to its lower RLT bound. In group-pair order
QQ, QH, QF, HH, HF, F F , the resulting squared distances are

                                  1/2,   7/8,    5/4,     5/4,   13/8,   2.

Pairs from groups with fewer than two members impose no extra requirement. This proves both
RLT values.



                                                     93
   For SDP, restrict to any k ≥ 2 points sharing [ℓ, ℓ + L] in one coordinate, and let e = 1 ∈ Rk .
With s = i (xi − ℓ), PSD and the secants give
        P


                       (Xii − 2Xij + Xjj ) = k tr X − eT Xe
                 X

                 i<j

                                          ≤ k[(2ℓ + L)        xi − kℓ(ℓ + L)] − (       xi )2
                                                         X                          X

                                                          i                         i
                                          = kLs − s ≤ k L /4.
                                                    2         2 2


The pair average per coordinate is at most kL2 /[2(k − 1)]. Add the two coordinates, first using
k = n, L = 1 and then the symmetry subset with L = 1/2, to obtain the two asserted SDP upper
bounds.
   Without symmetry, set x = y = e/2 and
                                                            n
                        X = Y = eeT /4 + Z,      Z=               (I − eeT /n) ⪰ 0.              (F.1)
                                                         4(n − 1)

Its diagonals equal 1/2 and its off-diagonals equal 1/4 − 1/[4(n − 1)]. Every pair has total distance
n/(n − 1). These entries also satisfy all RLT bounds.
    For symmetry use the same three group means as above. In each coordinate set the moment
matrix to mean outer product plus a covariance that is block diagonal in Q, H, F . On Q use in both
coordinates
                                               k
                                     ZQ =            (I − eeT /k).
                                           16(k − 1)
On H use I/16 in x and I/4 in y; on F use I/4 in both. These matrices are PSD because I − eeT /k
is an orthogonal projection. Every diagonal equals its tightened secant. The group-pair distances
are
                                   k        1   3     5     7
                                         ,    ,   ,     ,     , 1.
                                4(k − 1)    2   4     8     8
They are at least the first value for every k ≥ 2. For RLT feasibility, cross-group and distinct
H, F entries are products of their means and hence obey the four planes. On Q an off-diagonal is
9/16 − 1/[16(k − 1)] ∈ [1/2, 5/8], exactly within the tightened RLT range at mean 3/4. Diagonal
secants satisfy their diagonal lower planes as above. Thus adding RLT cannot change either SDP
optimum.

    In dimension d, the same one-coordinate average and the same covariance in each coordinate
give RLT value d and SDP value dn/[2(n − 1)] on [0, 1]d , for n ≥ 2. A subset of k ≥ 2 points
in a common side-L cube forces an SDP bound at most dL2 k/[2(k − 1)]. This includes L = 0.
These are dimension-scaled averaging statements, not claims about all symmetry or ordering models.
Numerical symmetry values at n < 5 and ordering formulations are not needed here. The benchmark
explains weakness of these particular root relaxations; it establishes no lower bound on every possible
strengthening or spatial algorithm.


G     Scale disjunctions and shared operating variables
Scaling gives an example where a representation can remove an artificial gap. The arguments are
affine interpolation and classical disjunctive convexification [5, Section 2, Theorem 2.1]; we give




                                                   94
them explicitly to identify the role of shared intensive variables. Let E ⊂ Rd × Rp be nonempty
compact, with per-unit extensive coordinates ξ and intensive coordinates T . Define

           λ ⊙ E = {(λξ, T ) : (ξ, T ) ∈ E},     F (Λ, E) = {(λ, λξ, T ) : λ ∈ Λ, (ξ, T ) ∈ E}.

The nonnegative scale set Λ is nonempty compact, with extremes λ− , λ+ . At zero scale, X = 0 and
T ∈ projT E; a different off-state domain must be modeled explicitly.

Proposition G.1 (Endpoint hull and multiplicities). With C = conv E, the hull of F (Λ, E) is the
hull of just its two extreme-scale slices, with E replaced by C. It is exactly the projection of

               0 ≤ θ ≤ 1,    λ = (1 − θ)λ− + θλ+ ,       X = X − + X +,    T = T − + T +,
                                                                                                    (G.1)
                      (X − , T − ) ∈ (1 − θ)(λ− ⊙ C),      (X + , T + ) ∈ θ(λ+ ⊙ C),

where 0K = {0} for nonempty K. For N ≥ 1 identical units sharing T , allow integer 0 ≤ n ≤ N ,
X = ni=1 ξi , and (ξi , T ) ∈ E. Their hull is the same as conv F ({0, . . . , N }, E). Writing PT =
      P

projT C, it is
                                                    n
             0 ≤ n ≤ N,        ∃T + : (X, T + ) ∈     (N ⊙ C),    T − T + ∈ (1 − n/N )PT .          (G.2)
                                                    N
Proof. For fixed (ξ, T ), the point (λ, λξ, T ) depends affinely on λ. If the extremes differ, interpolate
with θ = (λ − λ− )/(λ+ − λ− ); if they coincide, there is only one slice. Every original point is
therefore a combination of endpoint points. A fixed-scale map is linear in (ξ, T ), so its hull is
that slice of C. Combining two compact convex slices gives (G.1), including zero weights. For an
integer n > 0 and fixed T , a sum of n points of ET lies in n conv ET , and identical copies include
nET . Equivalently, a multiplicity point is the average of its n same-scale points (n, nξi , T ), while
every same-scale point is a feasible multiplicity point. Their hulls therefore agree at fixed n after
convexification. Taking the hull over n and applying the endpoint result proves (G.2). At n = 0
both models use the stated projection off-state; N = 0 consists only of that slice.

   If C = {(ξ, T ) : Aξ + BT ≤ b} and PT = {T : DT ≤ e} are polytopes, (G.2) becomes

                AX + N BT + ≤ bn,          D(T − T + ) ≤ e(1 − n/N ),        0 ≤ n ≤ N.

Compact conic-representable slices can also be homogenized at zero weight. Indeed, if K0 = {z :
∃y, Az + By + b ∈ K} is nonempty compact, then at weight t > 0 division in Az + Bw + tb ∈ K
gives tK0 . At t = 0 a feasible (z, w) is a recession direction of the lifted set: add any nonnegative
multiple to a feasible lift. Its projected direction must be zero by compactness of K0 . Conversely
z = w = 0 is feasible. This checks zero weights even when the auxiliary lift is unbounded. The
aggregation mechanism also appears in Wu et al. [41, Section 4.1, Lemma 3 and Theorem 3, under
Assumption 1].
    For example, let per-unit load ξ ∈ [ℓ, u], 0 ≤ ℓ < u, common T ∈ [a, b], a < b, and per-unit
extensive output ω = ξT . Then C is the McCormick hull of this bilinear graph. Formula (G.2)
supplies its exact local aggregate hull with one disaggregated intensive coordinate. Its endpoint
vertices are the two off-state points and the four corners at scale N . Besides nℓ ≤ X ≤ nu,
0 ≤ n ≤ N , a ≤ T ≤ b and the global McCormick inequalities for W = XT on [0, N u] × [a, b], this
hull has

            W ≤ bX + ℓ[N T − N a − n(b − a)],            W ≥ aX + ℓ[N T − N b + n(b − a)].          (G.3)


                                                    95
To check sufficiency without a facet calculation, introduce S = N T + . The scaled per-unit McCormick
system is equivalent to

                      an ≤ S ≤ bn,     N T − b(N − n) ≤ S ≤ N T − a(N − n),

       ℓS + aX − aℓn ≤ W ≤ ℓS + bX − bℓn,                  uS + bX − bun ≤ W ≤ uS + aX − aun.
Eliminating S by comparing each lower and upper bound gives precisely the displayed system
(redundant comparisons follow from the stated bounds). When ℓ = 0, use these inequalities directly
without dividing by ℓ. Alternatively (G.2) itself is a complete formulation at that boundary.

G.1    When scale costs do and do not separate
Example G.2 (A scale-cost gap on a convex per-unit set). Take E = {(ξ, T ) : ξ = T ∈ [0, 1]},
Λ = {0, 1, 2}, and f (0) = f (2) = 1, f (1) = 0. The point (λ, X, T ) = (1, 1, 1/2) is the average of
(0, 0, 0) and (2, 2, 1). Its true minimum convexified cost is one although the separate lower scale-cost
envelope has fˇ(1) = 0. In any representation let pi be the mass at scale i. Mean scale one gives
p0 = p2 . On scales zero, one, and two, respectively, X − T is at most zero, zero, and one. Thus the
target X − T = 1/2 forces p2 ≥ 1/2, and the cost is at least p0 + p2 ≥ 1. The endpoint representation
attains it. Scaling f scales this error arbitrarily.
   An operating cost must also be lifted before convexifying a nonconvex per-unit set. With
E = {−1, 1}, scale one and cost |ξ|, its convexified cost at X = 0 is one, not |0|. Further, λc(X/λ, T )
need not be convex with an unscaled intensive coordinate: the convex cost c(ξ, T ) = T 2 gives λT 2 .
Proposition G.3 (Extensive-only perspective). Let E ⊂ Rd be nonempty compact, c : E → R
continuous, and Λ ⊂ [0, ∞) finite and nonempty. Let h be the lower boundary of conv{(ξ, c(ξ)) :
ξ ∈ E} and fˇ the lower convex envelope of the finite scale-cost data. For λ > 0, the exact hull of
Z ≥ λc(ξ) + f (λ), X = λξ, is

                       λ ∈ conv Λ,    X ∈ λ conv E,           Z ≥ λh(X/λ) + fˇ(λ).

If 0 ∈ Λ, its zero-scale slice is X = 0, Z ≥ f (0).
Proof. For a representation with weights µj and positive mean scale λ, the size-biased weights
µj λj /λ sum to one. They give per-unit cost at least h(X/λ), while the original weights give scale
cost at least fˇ(λ). Conversely choose attaining per-unit atoms with weights νi and attaining scale
weights µj . Their product weights µj νi attain both bounds and the required X. Compactness
gives finite attaining representations; adding vertical slack gives every larger Z. At zero mean scale,
nonnegativity forces all positively weighted scales to be zero.

   This is the usual perspective argument after the cost has been included in the per-unit graph.
The multiplicity statement follows by treating that cost as another additive extensive coordinate.
Proposition G.4 (Rectangularity criterion). Let E ⊂ Rd × Rp be nonempty compact and let the
finite catalogue Λ ⊂ [0, M ] satisfy {0, s, M } ⊆ Λ and 0 < s < M = max Λ. Put C = conv E,
Q = projξ C, and PT = projT C. Every scale-only cost separates, in the precise sense

                     conv{(λ, X, T, Z) : (λ, X, T ) ∈ F (Λ, E), Z ≥ f (λ)}
                                                                                                  (G.4)
                          = {(λ, X, T, Z) : (λ, X, T ) ∈ conv F (Λ, E), Z ≥ fˇ(λ)},
if and only if C = Q × PT . It suffices to test the single cost f (s) = 0, f (λ) = 1 at every other
catalogue scale.

                                                      96
Proof. The epigraph hull equals the compact hull of the cost graph plus the nonnegative vertical ray.
Hence all required minimum-cost representations are attained; no closure operation is hidden. If the
diagnostic cost separates, every point of the scale-s base-hull slice has a zero-cost representation.
All its constituents must have scale s, so this slice is exactly s ⊙ C. Put α = s/M ∈ (0, 1). For
(ξ, T1 ) ∈ C and T0 ∈ PT , mixing the extreme slices gives (s, sξ, αT1 + (1 − α)T0 ) in that slice. Thus
the fibre of C at ξ contains αT1 + (1 − α)T0 . Iteration gives αj T1 + (1 − αj )T0 in the fibre for every
j. Closedness puts T0 in it, proving rectangularity. Conversely, under rectangularity the base hull
is {(λ, X, T ) : 0 ≤ λ ≤ M, X ∈ λQ, T ∈ PT }. For λ > 0 choose ξ = X/λ and use any attaining
scale-cost weights with the same (ξ, T ) ∈ C in every scale slice. Decompose that point into E within
each slice; the resulting representation attains fˇ(λ). At zero choose any ξ ∈ Q and use the zero
slice. Every representation has cost at least fˇ by convexity, proving equality. Universal separation
implies the diagnostic case, completing the equivalence.

    Without an interior scale, two endpoint weights are already fixed by λ, so all scale-only costs
separate even for nonrectangular C. Affine scale costs always separate. The criterion concerns all
scale-only costs with the stated off-state domain, not arbitrary operating costs.

G.2    Integral counts and aggregate rounding
For nonempty compact extensive-only unit types Ej ⊂ Rd and an integral polytope P ⊂ Rk+ of
allowable counts, the exact aggregate hull is

                                {(n, X) : n ∈ P, X ∈     j nj conv Ej }.                           (G.5)
                                                        P


Indeed it is convex and contains every integer multiplicity point. For the reverse inclusion write
X = j nj ξj with ξj ∈ conv Ej , decompose n into integral vertices of P , and keep these ξj fixed. At
      P

each integral vertex the sum of scaled convex hulls is the hull of the corresponding finite Minkowski
sum. This proves (G.5); no integer-decomposition property beyond integrality of P is needed.
    For n identical compact extensive sets in Rd , the classical Shapley–Folkman lemma [38, Appendix
2, Lemma 2 and corollary] represents any X ∈ n conv E as a sum with at most min(d, n) constituents
outside E. Replacing each exceptional constituent by any point of E changes the sum by at most
min(d, n) diam(E) in a chosen norm. Thus the same bound holds for the Hausdorff distance
between the two aggregate sets. An L-Lipschitz objective has gap at most L min(d, n) diam(E)
when minimized over those sets alone. A relative O(d/n) bound additionally requires a positive
normalization of order n and uniformly bounded L and diameter.
    All these identities concern the stated local or aggregate sets. Added balance constraints can
exclude the rounded point, and conv(F ∩ H) can be strictly smaller than conv F ∩ H. Endpoint
hull equality also does not license removing intermediate catalogue choices from the feasible model.
If the per-unit set depends on scale, even the hull equality can fail: ξ = λ at scales 1, 2, 3 gives
(λ, X) = (2, 4) outside the segment joining (1, 1) and (3, 9).


H     Retained domains and coordinate dependence in P-split
Kronqvist, Misener, and Tsay’s P-split construction splits convex additive constraints by groups
of variables, introduces epigraph auxiliaries for the groups, and convexifies the resulting bounded
auxiliary disjunction [29, Sections 2–4]. The original domain x ∈ X remains a global constraint.
The bounds are computed over this retained compact convex X, and identical or scaled group
functions share an auxiliary under the paper’s minimal-sharing Assumption 3 and Remark 1. In the


                                                   97
following comparisons, “basic” means these epigraph links, exact global interval bounds, and the
exact auxiliary disjunction hull. Extra original–auxiliary links change this model.

H.1     A domain counterexample and a local nonexactness criterion
The published Definition 4 calls disjuncts fully disjoint when they are pairwise disjoint. Theorem 6
states universal nonexactness when the bounds are additive and the constraint functions strictly
convex. Its universal statement fails with the retained-domain convention; this concerns that
theorem’s scope, not validity of the P-split formulations.
Example H.1 (An exact retained-box relaxation). On X = [0, 3] × [−1, 1], use
            D0 = {(t, w) ∈ X : t2 + w2 ≤ 1},        D3 = {(t, w) ∈ X : (t − 3)2 + w2 ≤ 1}.
The disjuncts are nonempty and disjoint, and are defined by strictly convex quadratics, with
additive component bounds on the box. Together they contain all four vertices of X. Hence
conv(D0 ∪ D3 ) = X, and every convex valid relaxation retaining X, including every P-split partition,
is exact. For a direct full-split lift use shared w2 and set
                                    (α0 , α3 , αw ) = (3t, 9 − 3t, 1).
This lies in the auxiliary hull as the combination of (0, 9, 1) and (9, 0, 1) with weights 1 − t/3, t/3.
The links follow from t2 ≤ 3t, (t − 3)2 ≤ 9 − 3t, and w2 ≤ 1. In dimension n ≥ 2 replace w2
by (n − 1)−1 ni=2 x2i on [0, 3] × [−1, 1]n−1 . The same box-vertex argument applies, with rational
              P

positive definite quadratics and one constraint per disjunct.
    The source explicitly calls its “few constraints” Assumption 4 technically unnecessary, and the
last construction also satisfies its intended large-dimension setting. In the proof of its Theorem 6,
strict Jensen slack is claimed in every component. Strict convexity supplies that slack only when
the two arguments differ. Along the top hull segment in the example, w is fixed at one. Further, a
neighborhood allowed by epigraph slack need not remain in X: an outward step through its top
facet is forbidden.
Proposition H.2 (A sufficient directional repair). Let X and Q be convex, A finite, and Fa
continuous convex. Define R = {x ∈ X : ∃α ∈ Q, Fa (x) ≤ αa (a ∈ A)}. Assume R contains
the entire true feasible set. Suppose true feasible points x0 , x1 have lifts F (x0 ), F (x1 ) ∈ Q. At
xθ = (1 − θ)x0 + θx1 , 0 < θ < 1, let ∆a = (1 − θ)Fa (x0 ) + θFa (x1 ) − Fa (xθ ). If a hull-supporting
inequality cT x ≤ β is tight at xθ and a direction v has cT v > 0, xθ + ϵv ∈ X for all small ϵ ≥ 0,
and Fa (xθ + ϵv) ≤ Fa (xθ ) for every zero-slack link and all small ϵ ≥ 0, then R strictly contains the
true hull.
Proof. Keep α = (1 − θ)F (x0 ) + θF (x1 ) ∈ Q. Every positive slack persists for a sufficiently small
step by continuity. There are finitely many links, so one common positive radius suffices. The
assumptions preserve the remaining links and domain, while the support violation is ϵcT v > 0. If
varying links have directional Lipschitz constants La on a common radius, imposing ϵLa ≤ ∆a for
every positive-slack link gives an explicit allowable step within that radius.

H.3     An unbounded coordinate effect with rational data
First take unit balls centered at 0 and (D, D), D ≥ 5, retaining [−1, D + 1]2 . The basic split
introduces four separate squares ai ≥ x2i , bi ≥ (xi − D)2 with upper bound U = (D + 1)2 , and
convexifies a1 + a2 ≤ 1 or b1 + b2 ≤ 1. At p = (2D/3, D/3) its true square vectors a, b have
2ai , 2bi ≤ 8D2 /9 ≤ U . Their average representation (a, b) = 12 (0, 2b) + 12 (2a, 0) proves feasibility in
the auxiliary
            √ hull. Projection onto the center segment is (D/2,√D/2), so the Hausdorff    √     error is at
least D/(3 2) − 1. Under the orthogonal change t = (x1 + x2 )/ 2, w = (x1 − x2 )/ 2, the retained

                                     √

                      √ is identical. Between centers the cylinder lies in the capsule. The new error

                                                       99
Proposition H.4 (Rational coordinate comparison). For rational D ≥ 2, unit balls with centers
0, v = (3D, 4D) in X = [−1, 3D + 1] × [−1, 4D + 1] have basic original-coordinate Hausdorff error
at least 4D/5 − 1. Under the fixed rational orthogonal map
                               t = (3x1 + 4x2 )/5,         w = (4x1 − 3x2 )/5,                           (H.4)
                                                                                                 √
using the exact transformed domain and tight recomputed bounds, the error is at most                 2 − 1.
Proof. The witness p = (2D, 4D/3) has each coordinate at one or two thirds of vi . Twice each
original square is at most 8vi2 /9 ≤ (vi + 1)2 , so the preceding half-weight lift is feasible. Its
transformed coordinates are t(p) = 34D/15 ∈ [0, 5D] and w(p) = 4D/5, proving the lower bound.
In aligned coordinates the common transverse auxiliary again forces |w| ≤ 1. For t ≤ 0, the
inverse map gives x1 ≤ 4/5 and x2 ≤ 3/5, while the retained domain gives x1 , x2 ≥ −1. Thus
t2 + w2 = x21 + x22 ≤ 2. For t ≥ 5D, apply the same argument to√x − v: its two components lie in
[−4/5, 1] and [−3/5, 1]. The error outside either end is at most 2 − 1, and between centers it is
zero. The map has determinant −1; changing the sign of w gives a proper rotation with identical
conclusions. All bounds and domain inequalities remain rational for rational D.

H.4     The exact auxiliary image is still different from the graph
Proposition H.5 (Strongest auxiliary-only comparison). In either original-coordinate example, let
F (x) = (x2i , (xi − vi )2 )2i=1 . Every convex auxiliary set Q containing F (0) and F (v) preserves the
original witness in
                                     R(Q) = {x ∈ X : ∃α ∈ Q, F (x) ≤ α}.
In particular the lower error survives Q = conv F (D0 ∪ Dv ). In aligned coordinates, however, the
exact feasible auxiliary image hull for F ′ (t, w) = (t2 , (t − d)2 , w2 ) gives exactly the true capsule, for
d > 2. This holds with either exactly transformed retained domain above.
Proof. Convexity forces [F (0)+F (v)]/2 ∈ Q, whose entries are vi2 /2. At the witness, both coordinate
squares are at most 4vi2 /9 and hence satisfy all epigraph links to that point. This argument uses
only the two feasible center images, so every valid convex auxiliary-only strengthening of the same
epigraph map retains the witness.
    For alignment write (a, b, c) = F ′ (t, w) and set
                                                     √
                            f (c) = d2 + 1 − c + 2d 1 − c,      0 ≤ c ≤ 1.
                                                                    √
At an actual feasible point, t ∈ [−q, q] ∪ [d − q, d + q] with q = 1 − c, so a, b ≤ (d + q)2 = f (c).
The function f is concave and decreasing. Its two hypographs therefore contain the whole convex
auxiliary image hull. If the epigraph links are feasible there, then |w| ≤ 1 and, by monotonicity,
                                       p                              p
                            |t| ≤ d + 1 − w2 ,       |t − d| ≤ d + 1 − w2 .
                                  √                    √
Their intersection is precisely − 1 − w2 ≤ t ≤ d + 1 − w2 , the capsule. Conversely the epigraph
relaxation is convex and contains both balls, so it contains their hull. The retained domain contains
that hull, which proves equality. In fact, just 0 ≤ c ≤ 1 and a, b ≤ f (c) suffice: each upper inequality
has a conic representation with s ≥ 0, s2 + c ≤ 1 and, respectively, a + c − d2 − 1 ≤ 2ds or
b + c − d2 − 1 ≤ 2ds.
    The exact auxiliary image hull retains only values of the chosen functions; it is not the hull of
their full feasible graph in (x, α). Original-space cuts, constraints coupling original and auxiliary
variables, or identities replacing the epigraph links can remove the witness. These calculations
establish explicit coordinate effects in this family, without an algorithm for choosing good coordinates
in general and without an assertion about every strengthened P-split formulation.

                                                     100
I     A stable correlation face and global lift size
This appendix compares global formulation size with the local gap and spatial resources in the
main text. All matrix ℓ1 norms here are entrywise sums, and ∥H∥∞ = maxij |Hij | for a cost matrix.
Scalar inequalities count as nonnegative cone factors.

I.1    The face and inherited exact lower bounds
For n ≥ 1 define the compact sets

              Rn = {W ≥ 0 : rank W ≤ 1, W e ≤ e, W T e ≤ e},           Kn = conv Rn .

For a nonzero generator, its row and column marginals r, c and total S satisfy W = rcT /S,
0 ≤ r, c ≤ e, and eT r = eT c = S ≤ n. Indeed, writing W = uv T and computing its marginals proves
this identity. Then tr W = rT c/S ≤ 1. Equality forces ri (1 − ci ) = ci (1 − ri ) = 0 for every i, so
r = c = 1A for a nonempty set A. Thus the trace-one face is the hull of 1A 1TA /|A|.

Proposition I.1 (Explicit correlation face). In K2m put
                                                                      m
                    T (W ) = e W e,    D(W ) = 1 − tr W,    B(W ) =
                                                                      X
                                 T
                                                                            Wi,m+i ,
                                                                      i=1

                                 F = {W ∈ K2m : D = B = 0, T = m}.
Then F is a face affinely isomorphic to COR(m) = conv{xxT : x ∈ {0, 1}m }. The forward map is
X = mW[m],[m] , and, with x = diag X, its inverse is

                               1
                                                                       !
                                    X        xeT − X
                           W =                             .                                     (I.1)

Moreover, the affine functional

                      g(W ) = m − T (W ) + (2m + 1)B(W ) + (4m + 1)D(W )

satisfies g ≥ B + D ≥ 0 on K2m and exposes exactly F .

Proof. Within the trace face, B = 0 selects sets A with at most one element from each pair {i, m + i}.
Their totals are |A| ≤ m; maximizing the total selects exactly one element per pair. These successive
faces give atoms (x, e − x)(x, e − x)T /m. Expanding their blocks gives (I.1), which extends affinely
to their hull. For exposure, on a nonzero generator the inequality |a − b| ≤ a(1 − b) + b(1 − a) gives
∥r − c∥1 ≤ 2SD. Also ri + rm+i − ri rm+i ≤ 1, so

                  S ≤m+          ri rm+i ≤ m + SB + ∥r − c∥1 ≤ m + 2mB + 4mD.
                            X

                             i

The zero generator satisfies the same affine inequality, hence so does K2m . Substitution proves
g ≥ B + D and the asserted zero set.

   Adding affine equations to a lift and composing its output map leaves its cone unchanged. Thus
every lift of K2m gives one of COR(m) over the same cone, without a strict-feasibility assumption.
The established correlation-polytope bounds therefore imply:


                                                 101
  • For fixed d and m ≥ d, a lift over (Sd+ )q has

                                   q ≥ (3d − 1)−(1−1/d) (1 − 3−d )−m/d .

      For d = 2 the stronger bound is q ≥ 7−1/2 (9/7)m/2 [20, Theorem 1].

  • The total dimension of an exact second-order-cone lift is at least 7−1/2 (9/7)m/2 . A Lorentz
    cone {(t, z) : ∥z∥2 ≤ t, z ∈ Rk } decomposes into k − 1 three-dimensional Lorentz cones for
    k ≥ 2, each isomorphic to S2+ ; one-dimensional nonnegative factors and the two-dimensional
    Lorentz cone require at most their dimension in such blocks. Apply the preceding bound.
                                                            2/13
  • An unrestricted PSD lift has matrix order at least 2αm for an absolute α > 0, for sufficiently
    large m [30, Theorem 5.4]. Several PSD blocks count by total order.

These are inherited conic lower bounds through the displayed face. They assert neither nonrep-
resentability by finitely many second-order cones nor an exponential number of Lorentz cones of
unrestricted dimension. For comparison, K2 is already nonpolyhedral: the face where its first row
and column sums equal one is the hull of W (t) = (1, t)T (1, t)/(1 + t), 0 ≤ t ≤ 1. With q = W11 its
lower-right entry is q + 1/q − 2, a strictly convex function on [1/2, 1]. Its infinitely many graph
points are extreme. Positive-error LP bounds below consequently carry information that exact finite
LP nonrepresentability alone does not.

I.2   A linear error bound and a quantitative section transfer
Proposition I.2 (Stability). For every W ∈ K2m ,

                                 dist1 (W, F ) ≤ (136m + 10)g(W ).                             (I.2)

                                      ≤ 4SB + 58SD + 5|S − m|.

Here the first two terms bound the comparison with ebebT /S, and its distance from V is |S − m|. Since
S ≤ 2m and |S − m| ≤ g + (2m + 1)B + (4m + 1)D, the last bound is at most (18m + 5)B + (136m +
5)D + 5g ≤ (136m + 10)g. For W = 0 the distance to any paired atom is m, while g(0) = 5m + 1.
Taking finite convex combinations proves (I.2).
    For the stronger transfer retain the bound 8mB + 116mD + 5|S − m| on each generator, including
zero. Exposure gives (S − m)+ ≤ 2mB + 4mD. In a convex decomposition of W , therefore,

                             λℓ |Sℓ − m| ≤ 4mB(W ) + 8mD(W ) + m − T (W ).

The corresponding combination V ∈ F consequently obeys

                      ∥W − V ∥1 ≤ 28mB(W ) + 156mD(W ) + 5[m − T (W )].

For Y ∈ Q ∩ HF choose W ∈ K2m with ∥Y − W ∥1 ≤ ϵ. The coefficient norms of B, D, T give
0 ≤ B(W ), D(W ) ≤ ϵ and m − T (W ) ≤ ϵ. The triangle inequality yields (184m + 6)ϵ. The block
projection has norm at most m, proving the last assertion.

   An immediate exact-penalty consequence is that for λ > (136m+10)∥H∥∞ , minimizing ⟨H, W ⟩+
λg(W ) over K2m has all minimizers in F and value minF ⟨H, V ⟩. Compare W with the point V
provided by (I.2); the excess is at least [λ − (136m + 10)∥H∥∞ ]g(W ).

I.3   Fine LP and SDP approximations
Proposition I.3 (Explicit accuracy scales). Let K2m ⊂ Q ⊂ K2m +ϵU1 with Q convex. If ϵ ≤ 1/Am ,
any LP lift of Q has 2Ω(m) inequalities. If ϵ ≤ 1/(2Am ), any PSD lift of Q has matrix order at least
exp(c(m/ log m)2/13 ) for an absolute c > 0 and all sufficiently large m.
Proof. For the LP assertion apply (I.3). The inequalities ⟨2 diag(a) − aaT , X⟩ ≤ 1, a ∈ {0, 1}m , have
coefficient norm at most one and slack (1 − aT x)2 at xxT . The projected set is therefore between
COR(m) and the polyhedron defined by these inequalities with right side two. The fixed-dilation
lower bound of Braun et al. [11, Theorem 6(i), author version p. 18] gives the result. This particular
sandwich has a small SDP relaxation and does not imply the next assertion.
     For that assertion use the sign correlation polytope SCOR(m) = conv{yy T : y ∈ {−1, 1}m }. We
first establish its fixed-error consequence of the pseudo-density bounds in Lee et al. [30, Theorem
3.8, equation (3.11), and Theorem 5.3]. For odd k ≥ 3 their construction supplies a degree-k
pseudo-density D on {0, 1}k with

                                                                               (   xi − k/2)2 − 1/4
                                                                               P
         ED = 1,    ∥D∥∞ ≤ k    3/2
                                      ,   EDfk = −1/(4k ),  2
                                                                    fk (x) =                        .
                                                                                        k2
Here degree-k means EDp2 ≥ 0 for deg p ≤ k/2. The cited quantitative theorem states that if
f : {0, 1}k → [0, 1] and EDf < −δ, then the matrix M (S, x) = f (xS ), indexed by k-subsets of [m]
and binary x, has, for m ≥ 2k, PSD rank at least

These pseudo-density and rank statements are established inputs.
   Suppose SCOR(m) ⊂ R ⊂ SCOR(m) + ηU1 with η ≤ 1/2. For every k-subset S the affine

If R has a PSD lift of order q, these valid affine slacks have a PSD factorization of order at most
q + 1. To justify this without a proper-lift assumption, restrict the lift to the smallest PSD face
containing its feasible set. A finite combination of feasible matrices spanning all feasible ranges is
positive definite on this face. Conic duality for the minimization of each nonnegative affine slack
then writes that slack on the affine slice as ⟨B, Y ⟩ + τ , B ⪰ 0, τ ≥ 0. Appending the scalar factors
τ and 1 supplies the asserted factorization. Choose odd k comparable to a(m/ log m)2/13 with a > 0
small enough that the bracket in (I.5) is at least 16. This proves the claimed superpolynomial order
for R.
    Finally partition W into four m-by-m blocks and set J(W ) = m(W11 − W12 − W21 + W22 ). Its
entrywise norm is at most m, and J(F ) = SCOR(m). The full-matrix transfer in Proposition I.2
shows that R = J(Q ∩ HF ) satisfies the preceding sandwich with η = Am ϵ ≤ 1/2. Affine sections
and images preserve the lift order, completing the proof.

    The scale in both assertions is explicit order m−2 . For compact convex outer sets this norm
guarantee is equivalent, by separation, to uniform additive objective error ϵ over all costs with
∥H∥∞ ≤ 1. It does not cover only one objective, a restricted cost family, or a larger accuracy
tolerance. No spatial region-count lower bound follows solely from these global lift-size bounds.
    At the same SDP accuracy scale, a second-order-cone lift also needs superpolynomial total cone
dimension: decompose its Lorentz cones into three-dimensional cones as above and embed them
block-diagonally into a PSD cone of order at most twice the original total dimension. This transfers
only the displayed superpolynomial approximate bound, whereas the exact second-order-cone bound
is exponential in m.


J    Limiting bounds and primitive FBBT updates
Feasibility-based bound tightening (FBBT) contracts a box by propagating individual constraints.
Its limit, the number of primitive updates, and the finite tolerance used by an implementation
are distinct objects. When the limiting box is nonempty, continuous linear FBBT limits can be
obtained by an LP [9, Theorem 4.1], although primitive iteration need not terminate finitely. Here
all variables start in [0, 1]. A primitive update computes the interval hull for one affine equality or one
nonnegative product equality and intersects it with the current box. For the limiting-bound theorem
we allow any additional sound contracting updates, provided every defining equation receives its
forward natural interval-arithmetic update infinitely often. This is fairness, with no bounded-delay
assumption. Exact real arithmetic specifies the mathematical limit; an approximation algorithm is
measured in the ordinary binary Turing model.
Lemma J.1 (Least fixed point transfer). Let P have nonnegative polynomial coefficients, and
suppose x = P (x) has a solution in the unit cube. Under sound contracting updates and fair forward
propagation, its limiting lower vector is the least nonnegative fixed point q of P . This includes lifted
arithmetic-node equations.
Proof. The sequence s0 = 0, sj+1 = P (sj ) increases and is bounded by every nonnegative fixed
point. Its limit q is a fixed point by continuity and is the least one. Soundness preserves q, so every

                                                   104
lower vector l satisfies l ≤ q. The natural interval lower endpoint is exactly Pi (l). Inductively, once
l ≥ sj , fairness supplies a finite later time at which all coordinates have received a forward update
and l ≥ sj+1 . Hence the lower limit is at least every sj and equals q. Apply the same argument to
the augmented polynomial system for an expression graph.

Proposition J.2 (Restricted limiting-bound hardness). Computing a designated limiting lower
bound to additive error 1/4 is PosSLP-hard, even for feasible affine and bilinear defining systems in
the unit cube with at most three variable names per equation, nonnegative coefficients in {1/2, 1},
constants in {0, 1/2, 1}, and distinct names in every product. Every strongly connected component
of the directed defining-equation dependency graph has at most four vertices. The feasible set has at
most two points, all rational.

Proof. PosSLP asks whether an integer arithmetic circuit using 0, 1, +, −, × has positive output.
Following the circuit normalization and amplification mechanism of Etessami and Yannakakis [19,
Theorem 5.2], replace every gate by positive and negative parts. Multiplication uses (uv)+ =
u+ v + + u− v − and (uv)− = u+ v − + u− v + ; addition and subtraction use the corresponding sums. It
suffices to compare two nonnegative integer outputs U, V .
    Pad these fan-in-two circuits to common depth L ≥ 1 with alternating addition and multiplication
layers, using identities v + 0, v · 1. This costs polynomial size. Define proof-only normalizers M0 = 1,
Mj = 2Mj−1 on addition layers and Mj = Mj−1        2  on multiplication layers. Encode each gate by its
normalized value p and complement r. At inputs they are zero or one; the equations for subsequent
gates are
                                     p = (pj + pk )/2, r = (rj + rk )/2
for addition and
                                   p = pj pk ,    v = pj rk ,   r = rj + v
for multiplication. These identities give p = value /Mj and r = 1 − p, with unique values in [0, 1].
Introduce a copy variable whenever a product would repeat a name. Each equation has at most
                                                                        L
three names and all these dependencies are acyclic. With M = ML ≤ 22 , put

               c = (pU + rV )/2 = 21 + (U − V )/(2M ),              d = (rU + pV )/2 = 1 − c.

The four-equation component

                                 a′ = a,     t = aa′ ,    v = ct,   a=d+v

implements a = d + ca2 . Its least nonnegative root a∗ equals one if c ≤ 1/2 and d/c if c > 1/2. In
the latter case, writing ∆ = U − V ≥ 1, 1 − a∗ = 2∆/(M + ∆) ≥ 1/M .
                       L+2
   Construct b = 2−2       and its complement by L + 2 paired squarings starting from 1/2. These
numbers require polynomially many equations, not their full rational expansions as input constants.
They satisfy b ≤ 1/(8M ). Add e = (1 − b)a, represented using the complement variable, and the
two-equation component w = ez, z = b + w. At the least detector root it has the unique solution
                                b
                   z∗ =                 =1     (U ≤ V ),        z∗ ≤ bM ≤ 1/8     (U > V ).
                          1 − (1 − b)a∗

All displayed values lie in the cube and form a fixed point. The least fixed point of the full system
has the unique acyclic values, the least detector root (bounded above by this displayed fixed point),
and then this unique final value. Lemma J.1 identifies z∗ with the FBBT limit. An additive-1/4
answer is at least 3/4 in the first case and at most 3/8 in the second; threshold 1/2 decides PosSLP.

                                                         105
The largest directed component has the four detector variables, and the last has two. The detector
has at most two rational roots, and each admissible root determines all other variables uniquely and
rationally.

    The directed graph has an edge from a defined variable to each argument on its right side; this
is not an undirected treewidth restriction. PosSLP is not known to be NP-hard. The proposition
does not assert hardness of executing a prescribed finite number of sweeps or of obtaining an
approximately stationary box. A unique rational feasible point is possible if one further affine
inequality is allowed: introduce y = ca and impose y ≤ 1/2. It retains the least detector root and
excludes a = 1 when c > 1/2; soundness and the fair original forward updates retain the same
limiting-bound proof.

Proposition J.3 (Every primitive schedule can be slow). For each n ≥ 1 a constant-coefficient
                                                                              n
affine–bilinear system with O(n) variables and equations requires at least 22 −1 applications of one
affine constraint before a designated lower bound reaches 1/2, although its limit is one. This holds
for every fair ordering of the bidirectional primitive interval-hull updates defined above. The only
feedback component has two variables and is linear after its upstream values are fixed.

Proof. Start with b0 = c0 = 1/2 and, for i = 1, . . . , n, impose

                       hi = bi−1 ,   bi = bi−1 hi ,   vi = bi−1 ci−1 ,   ci = ci−1 + vi .
                                                n
These acyclic equations give b = bn = 2−2 , c = cn = 1 − b. Add w = cz, z = b + w. The unique
feasible values are z = 1, w = c. For a stronger initialization fix all acyclic variables exactly, leaving
z, w ∈ [0, 1]. Primitive interval-hull contractors are monotone under box inclusion, so the original
run with the same schedule has no larger lower bounds.
    In the stronger run the invariant is 0 ≤ lw ≤ clz , lz ≤ 1. A product update sets lw to clz at
most; its inverse bound lz ≥ lw /c cannot improve lz . An affine update sets

                     lznew = max(lz , b + lw ) ≤ b + clz ,       new
                                                                lw   = max(lw , lz − b).

Since lz − b − clz = b(lz − 1) ≤ 0, both terms defining the new lower bound on w are at most
clznew . This proves the invariant, including simultaneous hull updates. Upper endpoints cannot
improve these formulas: feasibility forces uz = 1, while uw is either one or c. After K affine updates,
induction and Bernoulli’s inequality give

                                     lz ≤ 1 − cK = 1 − (1 − b)K ≤ Kb.
                                            n
Thus lz ≥ 1/2 requires K ≥ 1/(2b) = 22 −1 .

    The precise bound is in the displayed parameter n; encoding indexed variable names as binary
strings gives ordinary input length O(n log n). At lz = lw = 0 with upstream variables fixed, every
primitive update changes any lower endpoint by at most b, although lz is distance one from its limit.
Small local residual and small distance to the limiting box are therefore different guarantees.
    Slow and nonfinite linear FBBT iteration is classical, as are repeated squaring and error
amplification. The slow-Newton examples of Esparza et al. [18, Section 7, Theorem 7.1] and Stewart
et al. [39, Section 4.1, equation (18)] also imply a doubly exponential first-bit bound for ordinary
Kleene iteration. This is an inference from their displayed systems, not their stated Newton theorem.
For Stewart et al.’s chain, x0 = (x20 + 1)/2 and xi = (x2i + xi−1 )/2, the errors from the fixed point
one satisfy e0 (k + 1) = e0 (k) − e0 (k)2 /2 and ei (k)2 ≥ ei−1 (k), the latter because each iterate is below
its next update. The first recurrence gives e0 (k) ≥ 1/(k + 1) by induction (the map is increasing

                                                      106
                                         n                           n
on [0, 1]). Hence en (k) ≥ (k + 1)−1/2 > 1/2 when k + 1 < 22 . The specific scope proved here is
every ordering of the stated bidirectional primitive contractors, with one feedback component that
becomes linear. Symbolic substitution, equation aggregation, fixed-point acceleration, and global
contractors are outside this iteration bound.


K     A short comparison with integer precision
Here the resource is the number p of integer coordinates in a convex lift, or binaries in a linear lift.
A relaxation contains the graph and has vertical error at most ϵ > 0 at every projected point over
its box. There is no bound on its continuous auxiliary dimension or on the ranges of general integer
coordinates. The published parity midpoint argument [31, Lemma 4.1] groups graph points by the
parity of a feasible integer lift. The midpoint of two lifts in one class has integer coordinates and
remains feasible by convexity. Taking closures of these at most 2p classes in the compact input box
preserves every continuous midpoint-error inequality and avoids any measurability assumption on
the original lift.
    For x2 on [0, 1], the midpoint error is (a − b)2 /4. Each closed class therefore has diameter at
       √                                        √
most 2 ϵ. Covering the interval gives 1 ≤ 2p 2 ϵ, or

                                                 ϵ ≥ 2−2p−2 .                                     (K.1)

This is attained by a binary linear relaxation: divide the interval into 2p equal intervals of length h.
On each interval use its secant as upper bound and the maximum of the endpoint tangents as lower
bound. Both vertical errors are at most h2 /4, with equality in the upper bound at the midpoint.
The finite union of these bounded polyhedra has a linear encoding with p binary labels (unused
auxiliary dimension and row count are unrestricted). Thus the minimum integer dimension and
the minimum binary count both equal max{0, ⌈(log2 (1/ϵ) − 2)/2⌉}. The more compact sawtooth
construction has the same exact worst error [7, combined 2022 preprint, Section 5.1.1, PDF p. 21];
that inspected preprint has different numbering from the published parts.
    For xy, the corresponding midpoint inequality is |(a1 − b1 )(a2 − b2 )| ≤ 4ϵ. A compact class S has
area at most 16ϵ log 2. Indeed choose points a, b attaining the extreme first coordinates, with width
X > 0. At first coordinate a1 + t its section is contained in intervals centered at a2 , b2 of lengths
                                                                   R X/2
8ϵ/t, 8ϵ/(X − t), respectively. Integrating their minimum gives 2 0 8ϵ/(X − t) dt = 16ϵ log 2. The
zero-width case has zero area. Hence
                                          1
                                                 ≤ ϵbest (p) ≤ 2−p−2 .                            (K.2)
                                     16(log 2)2p
For the upper bound partition the first coordinate into 2p strips and use the four McCormick
inequalities in each. On a rectangle of side lengths h, k their maximum vertical error is hk/4, as
follows by scaling the unit-square formulas. Thus both general integer dimension and binary count
are log2 (1/ϵ) + O(1) for this scalar product.
    The simultaneous graph wij = xi xj , ij ∈ E(G), has a different leading coefficient: the fractional
vertex-cover number

                         τ ∗ (G) = min{       i ai : ai ≥ 0, ai + aj ≥ 1 (ij ∈ E)}.
                                          P

Then the least general integer dimension and binary linear count obey

For completeness, if a compact planar set satisfies |∆x∆y| ≤ 4ϵ, its coordinate widths satisfy
dx dy ≤ 20ϵ: two extreme-x points differ in y by at most 4ϵ/dx , and every other point is within
8ϵ/dx in y of one of them. Apply this to each edge projection of a closed parity class. If all its
widths di are positive, si = − log2 di is feasible for L20 , so its volume is at most i di ≤ 2−L20 ; a
                                                                                     Q

zero width gives zero volume. The cover proves the lower bound.
    For the upper bound take an optimum s of L4 , put pi = ⌈si ⌉, and share the binary expansion
xi = pk=1  2−k βik + ri , 0 ≤ ri ≤ 2−pi , across every incident product. For each edge, expand first
      P i

in xi and then expand the residual term in xj . Binary–continuous products have exact four-row
linear descriptions; only ri rj needs a McCormick relaxation. Its error is at most 2−pi −pj /4 ≤ ϵij .
This proves (K.3) with at most O(|V | + |E| + i deg(i)pi ) continuous variables and rows. For fixed
                                                 P

nonempty G and common error tending to zero, LC = τ ∗ (G) log2 (1/(Cϵ)). Consequently

                       pmin (ϵ) = pbin (ϵ) + OG (1) = τ ∗ (G) log2 (1/ϵ) + OG (1).

These arguments count binary assignments or integer parity classes. Their feasible midpoint belongs
to the mixed-integer projection; it need not be a region certified by any spatial bounding oracle.
